Cardinale: "Vogliamo vincere e servire i tifosi. Con MSE possiamo portare a Milano il meglio dello sport e dell'intrattenimento dal vivo americani"

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

RedBird ha una consolidata esperienza nella creazione e nello sviluppo di aziende nei settori dello sport, dei media e dell'hospitality (Legends Hospitality, On Location, YES Network, EverPass).

La neonata Milan Sports & Entertainment (“MSE”), holding che riunirà gli investimenti di RedBird a Milano nei settori dello sport, delle infrastrutture e dell'intrattenimento, metterà a frutto le competenze di RedBird per costruire una piattaforma il cui valore complessivo, le opportunità commerciali e l'influenza globale vadano ben oltre quelli delle singole realtà che la compongono.

Gerry Cardinale, Founder e Managing Partner di RedBird Capital, ha dichiarato: “Negli ultimi 25 anni abbiamo lavorato al fianco dei rights holder nei settori dello sport e dei media per creare imprese innovative che hanno trasformato i rispettivi settori. Questa evoluzione si fonda su un profondo rispetto per il patrimonio di proprietà intellettuale alla base di queste realtà, su una partnership di lungo periodo con i titolari dei diritti e sull'ambizione di valorizzare appieno il potenziale di queste squadre e leghe, dentro e fuori dal campo. Milan Sports & Entertainment rappresenta il risultato di questo percorso e del lavoro portato avanti da quando, quattro anni fa, abbiamo investito per la prima volta in AC Milan. Diventare proprietari di un club ci ha fatto comprendere ancora più a fondo ciò che crea valore nel lungo periodo: vincere, essere al servizio dei tifosi e investire nelle comunità che danno significato a queste istituzioni. Oggi applichiamo questa esperienza su una scala diversa: non a un solo club, a un solo sport o a un solo impianto, ma a un'intera città e a un portfolio che comprende sport, lifestyle e intrattenimento. Conosco Brett Yormark e lavoro con lui da 25 anni, e non riesco a immaginare un dirigente migliore nel settore dello sport internazionale per contribuire a realizzare questa strategia. Grazie a Brett, possiamo portare a Milano il meglio dello sport e dell'intrattenimento dal vivo americani, contribuendo a scrivere un nuovo capitolo nel percorso di crescita e affermazione internazionale della città. Con il Milan come punto di riferimento, che sarà sempre la forza trainante di tutte le nostre iniziative, intendiamo costruire una piattaforma dello sport e dell'intrattenimento di livello mondiale, capace di plasmare il futuro di Milano e dello sport europeo e di riportarlo ai vertici della scena globale”.

Nvidia CEO steps down. And, Sen. Chris Van Hollen to run for majority leader

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is stepping down following reports of his extramarital affairs, the company's board announced. The company's chief financial officer, Colette Kress, has been appointed interim CEO. The company launched an investigation after allegations surfaced that Huang had an affair with one of its clients, who U.S. intelligence officials concluded had been recruited by China's top spy agency. In an email to Nvidia staff, Huang admitted to cheating on his wife and promised to cooperate with the investigation. Nvidia's share price fell sharply Wednesday after the news of his second affair with an Nvidia executive came to light. Huang was widely considered an important business leader in the U.S. and had close ties to President Trump.

Jensen Huang gives a keynote address at the Nvidia Berlin GPU Technology Conference on Oct. 21, 2026. Sean Gallup/Getty Images hide caption

White House envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner have been busy this week working on a new security framework between Russia and Europe and seeking to end the war in Ukraine. They led peace talks in Abu Dhabi, met with Russian President Vladimir Putin in Moscow and then traveled to Ireland to meet with Ukraine's president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy.

Sen. Chris Van Hollen of Maryland has announced he will challenge New York's Chuck Schumer for the Senate majority leader position. Following recent midterm victories, Van Hollen said voters desired a "change from the status quo." Elected to the Senate in 2016 after more than a decade in the House, the 67-year-old progressive gained national attention last year for lobbying for the release of Kilmar Abrego Garcia after his wrongful deportation to a prison in El Salvador. His announcement comes as progressive activists and some Democrats push for the 75-year-old Schumer's replacement. Schumer has been in the role since 2017 but faced criticism from the Democratic base for failing to confront Trump vigorously during a funding dispute in early 2025.

JPMorgan Buys More Strategy and Bitcoin ETF Despite MSCI Saylor Blow

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

JPMorgan is quietly increasing its Bitcoin exposure while the crypto market is being beaten up. The banking giant raised its Strategy holdings by 36% and added another 1 million shares of BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT).

JPMorgan Bitcoin positioning makes an interesting contrast with the current market panic. Bitcoin has plunged below $70,000, wiping out leveraged bullish bets and dragging major altcoins lower. Meanwhile, Michael Saylor’s Strategy faces another institutional headache after MSCI confirmed its removal from major equity indexes.

Wall Street might be seeing something that the market is missing. JPMorgan’s latest holdings show institutional exposure was growing before the latest sell-off, even as Strategy faces mounting pressure.

Could this be smart money positioning for Bitcoin’s next recovery?

JPMorgan held 848,932 Strategy shares worth $131.3 million at the end of September. That’s a 36% increase from its 624,387 shares reported three months earlier.

Its exposure to BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF grew even further in dollar terms. The bank reported 11.4 million IBIT shares worth $540 million, compared with 10.4 million shares valued at $355.7 million in June.

The IBIT position increased by 9.6% in share count, while its reported market value jumped 52%. JPMorgan also reported smaller positions in other Bitcoin funds, including Grayscale’s Bitcoin Mini Trust and Bitwise’s Bitcoin ETF.

Together, the Strategy and IBIT positions were worth $671 million at September 30. That’s a sizeable institutional footprint across two different ways of gaining Bitcoin exposure.

However, the timing makes this filing particularly interesting. These holdings paint JPMorgan’s third quarter, ending September 30. JPMorgan was building exposure before Bitcoin’s latest breakdown, leaving investors wondering if the bank still sees value in the correction.

There is another detail worth watching. JPMorgan also disclosed substantial put-option exposure against Strategy and IBIT, alongside call positions. Its derivatives book shows a more complicated positioning strategy than a straightforward bullish bet. Still, the increased share counts show that the bank expanded its underlying holdings.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

JPMorgan’s growing Strategy position comes at an awkward moment for Saylor. MSCI confirmed that Strategy will leave its MSCI USA Index following the November 30 market close. The company is also losing its place in the MSCI World and ACWI benchmarks as the index provider implements new eligibility rules for non-operating companies.

JUST IN: MSCI to officially remove Michael Saylor's Strategy $MSTR from its indexes by the end of this month. pic.twitter.com/h1jBFtVU8M

This decision could create another source of selling pressure. Passive funds tracking affected benchmarks generally need to adjust their holdings when constituents disappear. Strategy previously warned that potential index exclusions could trigger billions of dollars in outflows.

JPMorgan previously estimated that MSCI exclusion alone could result in $2.8 billion in selling pressure. The figure could climb substantially if other major index providers follow the same approach.

Yet JPMorgan increased its Strategy holdings during the quarter leading into this decision. That creates an interesting contradiction: one of Wall Street’s largest banks expanded its exposure while the company was heading toward a major index setback.

Bitcoin slipped below $69,000 this morning, its lowest level since August, while crypto liquidations reached $1.6 billion over 24 hours. More than $1.3 billion came from liquidated long positions, highlighting just how aggressively leveraged traders were caught on the wrong side of the move.

This selling can create opportunities for investors with longer time horizons. When leveraged positions disappear, weaker hands are forced out, leaving more room for institutional accumulation once volatility settles.

The MSCI setback, no doubt, creates a potential disconnect between index-driven selling and longer-term investment demand.

Universal's Best Twilight / Harry Potter Replacement Has Been In Development Hell For Six Years

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Step aside, Twilight, and move over, Harry Potter; Universal has the perfect answer to both. It may seem an unlikely combination; Twilight is a "sparkly vampire" romance, while Harry Potter blazed the trail for the current generation of magic schools (complete with a healthy dose of a Chosen One destiny). And yet, have you ever noticed how the best original stories are a delightful mix of multiple ideas, creating something refreshingly new and yet delightfully familiar?

Step forward Tracy Wolff's Crave, which is a stunning blend of the two. It stars Grace Foster, a young orphan who winds up sent to what turns out to be a school for vampires, werewolves, dragons, witches, and more. And, of course, the different groups don't get on at all. The book is delightfully self-aware (at one point the vampire love-interest gives Grace a gift copy of Twilight), and it turns so many of the central tropes upside-down in a fantastic way.

Crave launched in 2020, and it's expanded into a full six-book series. Katmere Academy isn't Hogwarts; it's much more dangerous, a little closer to the dark academia books I've fallen in love with of late. The various factions initially feel almost like your traditional high school, where the jocks and the nerds all hang out together, but it soon becomes clear the racial "tribes" are much more dangerous. Grace has no idea what she's dealing with, and initially there's an entertaining sense of denial as readers figure out what's happening even as she tries to tell herself she's going nuts.

That said, my personal favorite twist here is that the heroes are not the Chosen Ones. In fact, this is a franchise where the Chosen One went wrong; where the vampire love interest's brother was believed to be a Chosen One, and was raised to believe he was something special. It's fascinating to realize how much this kind of philosophy can twist and scar a person, making the Crave story a fun interrogation of a trope we've seen played out so many times. It reminds me a little of Anakin Skywalker in the Star Wars prequels, but a little better done (with apologies to George Lucas).

As a lover of Twilight and Harry Potter, and someone who very much enjoys romantasy, I can honestly say Crave is a must-read. Wolff is wonderfully creative, and her world is so very immersive; even when she plays with familiar tropes and character beats, she does so with a nod and a wink. If you like vampires and werewolves, magic schools, or even dragon shifters, this is the series for you.

But here's the annoying thing; Universal acquired the rights to Crave before the first book even came out, with Universal’s SVP of Production Jeyun Munford and Creative Executive Christine Sun set to oversee the production. We've had zero news about the film since then, though, which is something of a surprise; unfortunately, Munford isn't even at Universal anymore, which may well mean this particular movie has been lost in development hell. If so, it's a real shame; at a time when both Harry Potter and Twilight are re-emerging, this is an epic that should really be given a chance to flourish.

For now, readers would be wise to pick up Crave for something that will definitely scratch that itch in the best possible way.

Angus Crichton Promoted to Head of Global Product at Scenic

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Scenic Group has promoted Angus Crichton to head of global product, bringing his experience across sales, marketing and product development to the leadership of its product teams for Scenic and Emerald Cruises & Tours.

Now based at the Group’s office in Zug, Switzerland, he will lead product development across luxury river cruising, yacht cruising and escorted land journeys, strengthening the connection between itinerary design and the guest experience.

“As the only company to design, build, own and operate our ships, we have a truly unique ability to innovate across every aspect of the guest experience, from the luxury spaces on board to the destinations we visit and the journeys we create,” said Sture Myrmell, chief operating officer, Scenic Group.

“That same philosophy connects our ocean, river and land journeys, giving Scenic and Emerald Cruises & Tours guests the confidence to explore in different ways while enjoying a seamless experience with the brands they know and trust. Angus’s deep understanding of our business makes him ideally placed to bring these strengths together as we expand our global portfolio.”

Crichton previously led marketing, sales and product for Emerald in Australia and New Zealand, alongside responsibility for the land product division across Scenic and Emerald.

“Having been part of Scenic Group’s evolution over more than 26 years, I’m excited to help drive its next chapter. Bringing product development and our commercial teams closer together will help us deliver exceptional journeys for our guests and give our travel partners compelling experiences to sell,” noted Crichton.

The appointment comes as Scenic Group progresses what it says is its most significant expansion program in decades, with eight new ships planned for 2027 and 2028 and further announcements to come across its luxury river and yacht fleets.

River fleet additions include the Emerald Lumi on France’s Seine River. The ship recently touched water for the first time.

Scenic is also planning expansion to India’s Hooghly River in 2027, with a further ship planned for India’s Brahmaputra River in 2028.

The Scenic Spirit II and Scenic Spirit III are set to debut on the Mekong River from 2028.

The three new luxury yachts comprise the Emerald Raiya in 2027, followed by the Emerald Xara and the Scenic Ikon in 2028.

The Ikon will debut in the Mediterranean in April 2028, with Antarctica featuring in its inaugural season.

The Love Is Blind Season 11 Reunion Was Awful, But That Seems On Par With The Season

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

The Gilded Age Fans Should Mind Their Business About Marian And Larry

After Watching The Invite As A Divorcee, I Have A Take On The Ending

90 Day Fiancé: The Other Way Just Hit On Andi And Nigel's Biggest Issue, And I Don't See Them Getting Over It

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Love Is Blind once again proves that love is not blind, or maybe shouldn’t be, with another disastrous season. However, this may be one of the show’s most unsuccessful seasons in terms of marriages and long-term relationships. The one marriage from the season didn’t even last a year.

An interesting reunion can usually redeem a less-than-exciting season of Love is Blind, which you can watch with a Netflix subscription. We have seen many do just that. However, this reunion confirmed just how disappointing Season 11 is. It magnified Love is Blind’s issues.

Warning: Love is Blind Season 11 reunion spoilers are ahead. Proceed with caution.

I have watched all 11 seasons of Love Is Blind, and the reunions are getting worse. These are supposed to be an opportunity to dig into some of the biggest unanswered questions. They could be a chance to hold people accountable for questionable behavior from previous episodes.

Hosts Nick and Vanessa Lachey achieve some of these things, but not fully. Rob Feeley had one of the season’s biggest moments. It led to many online discussions about his and his friends’ problematic behavior. The reunion barely addresses it.

Other things aren’t fully addressed, including details of some of the breakups. For example, we’re left confused about Michaella Giorgio and Matt Waldrip’s failed relationship. We leave the season with a lot more questions than answers.

The whole point of the reunion is to get answers and closure for the viewers. Even the messiest seasons usually have some bright spots, but Love Is Blind: Boston doesn’t seem to have any. This makes us worry even more about the series’ future.

The reunion had bromance montages, an engagement announcement from someone who was barely on the season, a focus on some men who were barely on the show, a look at Blake Carver’s life in the sea, and a musical number. It seemed like the Love Is Blind Season 11 reunion wanted to do everything but focus on what viewers care about: information about the couples.

I know the show wanted to balance some lighthearted moments with the drama and heaviness. However, they just felt out of place. It took away from the really interesting parts of the reunion. Additionally, it felt extremely ironic that a show that’s supposed to focus on proving love can work under extreme circumstances chose to have “Stop the Wedding!” by Ashe as the season’s theme song.

It further emphasizes how much Love Is Blind doesn’t take itself seriously anymore. We don’t know if the producers still care at all about having successful couples.

Automatically, the Love Is Blind reunion felt uncomfortable. It just became increasingly that way as it progressed. It was an intense season, but the producers could have done more to make the tension less intense. It was either too intense or ridiculous with showing unnecessary things like montages.

Love Is Blind has had plenty of iconic moments, so the show has the capability for that. However, it’s losing its way in producing a well-executed, compelling show about relationships. The season as a whole and the reunion highlights this problem. Hopefully, they can start to pull it back to its old glory in the upcoming season.

We still think Love Is Blind, but it could use some help.

New York Times Blames Trump For Air Safety Risks—Gets Blasted By The Independent NTSB

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The National Transportation Safety Board says the New York Times distorted its comments to attack President Trump. The paper’s aviation safety story has bigger problems than just politics. It gets a major crash wrong and credits a pilot hours rule for a safety record that began years before the rule took effect. James Fallows’s “Is Air Travel Getting Less Safe?” describes real issues of overworked air traffic controllers, military aircraft operating around passenger flights, and safety recommendations that haven’t been adequately addressed. Then it tries to turn them into a story about Trump dismantling the institutions that keep us safe, even though the issues he cites have been going on far longer than the current administration. I’ve written predominantly critically about Trump since he first ran for president 11 years ago. I rejected his effort to blame diversity hiring for the American Airlines 5342 tragedy before investigators had established what happened. That doesn’t make every argument against him a good one. The Times introduces Fallows as an instrument-rated private pilot with more than 2,000 hours. That’s true but while he’s written extensively about aviation it’s an odd way to frame him as author of this story. He’s also Jimmy Carter’s former chief speechwriter, a longtime Atlantic writer and editor, and a familiar voice on NPR. His career has been about politics and public policy. His own introduction to the article frames the piece as meant to connect aviation under Trump with public health, international aid, diplomacy, higher education and the rule of law. Aviation is the vehicle for a larger political point. The piece isn’t really about aviation, it’s using aviation. The Independent NTSB Attacks The Piece The NTSB says the article: Distorts the agency’s responses with political statements intended to attack the current administration. Mischaracterizes the NTSB’s role in the 1,500 hour pilot requirement. Incorrectly implies negligence by the Black Hawk pilots involved in the Washington National Airport collision. The statement also thanks the administration for supporting the agency and providing resources. That last part is political messaging too. (Gratitude for a budget doesn’t answer address aviation safety, but is probably necessary as a defensive measure.) “We are disappointed that this article sensationalizes and politicizes tragedies.” The NTSB’s response to the New York Times Magazine. — NTSB Newsroom (@NTSB_Newsroom) November 12, 2026 Fallows interviewed NTSB Chair Jennifer Homendy. The agency argues she’s being mischaracterized, but they don’t actually say how. In the article she says, “Recommendations alone don’t save lives. Action does.” Her own July statement about the Washington crash warned that delays implementing the board’s recommendations represented a continuing, unacceptable risk. The Article Gets The Colgan Air Crash Wrong Fallows spends considerable space describing the 2009 Colgan Air crash near Buffalo – as the last U.S. airline crash with loss of life before American 5342. In fact, PenAir flight 3296 overran the runway at Unalaska in October 2019, killing a passenger. His account has the Colgan Air plane accumulating ice until it becomes a “frigid brick.” He writes that by the time the captain recognized the problem, it “might already have become unflyable.” But that isn’t what the investigation found. The FAA’s accident analysis says icing wasn’t a cause. When the stall warning activated, the airplane wasn’t yet stalled. The captain pulled back on the controls and put it into a stall. The crew had failed to monitor their airspeed and mishandled the approach. And both Colgan pilots already had more than 1,500 hours: The captain had 3,379 hours. The captain had a history of training failures despite the hours. The first officer had 2,244 hours, including 1,470 hours as a light airplane flight instructor and 774 hours in turbine aircraft. The 1,500 Hour Rule For Co-Pilots Isn’t The Reason For The Safety Improvements After Colgan, Congress and regulators made several changes. Pilot rest rules improved. Training changed. Pilot qualification requirements changed. Sharing pilot records improved. Evidence that safety has performed well after a package of changes doesn’t establish that (1) every element of those changes (2) caused the improvements. And there’s strong reason to believe the the 1,500 hour rule has the opposite effect. It makes becoming a pilot take far longer and become far more costly, which keeps talented people from becoming pilots. Those with a higher opportunity cost, or lacking access to resources needed, get excluded from the pool of potential pilots. You’re then drawing from a smaller group. The NTSB didn’t recommend the 1,500 hour number. In 2010 Senate testimony, then-chair Deborah Hersman explained that the board had investigated accidents involving both high-time and low-time pilots. It found a relationship between failed practical tests and subsequent accidents, but there wasn’t evidence that number of hours actually mattered. The higher minimum took effect in 2013. Fallows’s safety streak begins in February 2009. The first four and a half years of that streak can’t be credited to a requirement that wasn’t yet in effect. Making pilots chase hours can allow relevant skills to deteriorate and bad habits to develop before an airline has to train those habits out of them. Repeated flights in clear weather, making takeoffs and landings at the same familiar airports, aren’t equivalent to structured training for instrument flying in a jet with another pilot, airline procedures, and complex air traffic control instructions. Hours in a hot air balloon count. The hot air balloon can be tethered. European co-pilots don’t face the same restriction, but their safety record is equivalent. Sub-1,500 hour European pilots fly in the United States every day without concern (and American pilots fly in Europe without elevated risk). The argument for the rule is that experience can build judgment, and that the higher threshold was part of a successful improvement in regional airline standards. But that’s an argument for useful experience and rigorous standards. That’s not what we got. An hour spent building time isn’t as valuable as an hour spent in a supervised airline training program. And there’s zero evidence that 1,500 is the right metric. It was chosen because it’s expensive and time-consuming, restricts entry into the pilot profession and drives up wages, and was supported by the major pilots union. The Black Hawk Crew Bears Responsibility The NTSB objected to Fallows implying negligence by the helicopter pilots. But its own probable cause finding identifies the helicopter crew’s failure to maintain effective visual separation as a cause of the collision. The crew accepted responsibility for avoiding the airliner and failed to do so. The existence of systemic failures don’t eliminate pilot error. In Homendy’s testimony explaining the findings, the board concluded that the helicopter’s altimeter probably understated its altitude by about 100 feet. The crew likely believed it was below the route’s ceiling. Fallows also describes the crew flying through civilian traffic without monitoring its traffic avoidance systems. The helicopter had no integrated cockpit traffic display. The pilots had tablets capable of showing traffic, but looking down at them while flying low at night with night vision goggles was problematic. Their audio alerts weren’t integrated into the pilots’ helmets. There were failures of FAA route design, and the FAA failed to act in the face of repeated warnings. But it’s genuinely weird to pin the blame for that on the first nine days of the Trump administration before the crash. I’ve criticized efforts to preserve military exceptions around Washington National. Civilian aviation shouldn’t have to accommodate unnecessary military risks. The Pentagon needs to change, and the current administration is responsible for making that happen. But it didn’t invent the culture that allowed these risks to exist in the first place. The Article’s Own Incident Data Doesn’t Support A Partisan Cause Fallows combines airline accidents, rocket debris, military encounters, airshow incidents and presidential flyovers which generally have different causes. And a list that attributes incidents while a given President is in office to that President doesn’t do a good job of evaluating what decisions caused those incidents. But more simply, even the article’s own figures for the most serious runway incursions are problematic for the Fallows narrative: 2023: 22 incidents. 2024: 7 incidents. 2025: 14 incidents. 2026 through October: 9 incidents. The increase from 2024 to 2025 looks dramatic – a doubling! But it’s still far lower than under Biden in 2023! These are relatively small numbers, and they don’t show a steadily worsening trend. (And there are more aircraft operations than in 2023.) Air Traffic Control Was Broken Before Trump Fallows points to deregulation and falling fares as sources of overcrowding. More people being able to afford to fly is a good thing, and flying is still safer than driving. The failure is an FAA air traffic organization system that hasn’t kept up with demand. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election. Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal. And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. I’ve criticized Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy for counting initial trainee hires as progress, that strikes me as juking the stats but hasn’t been a safety issue. And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place. I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider. (HT: Paddle Your Own Kanoo) Topics on this pageAviation safetyDonald TrumpJames FallowsNational Transportation Safety BoardThe New York TimesAir traffic controlAmerican AirlinesBuffaloCanadaColgan AirColgan Air Flight 3407Federal Aviation AdministrationJimmy CarterJoe BidenNational Public RadioRonald Reagan Washington National AirportSean DuffyThe TimesUnited States CongressUnited States Senate+15 more More From View from the Wing

James Fallows’s “Is Air Travel Getting Less Safe?” describes real issues of overworked air traffic controllers, military aircraft operating around passenger flights, and safety recommendations that haven’t been adequately addressed. Then it tries to turn them into a story about Trump dismantling the institutions that keep us safe, even though the issues he cites have been going on far longer than the current administration.

I’ve written predominantly critically about Trump since he first ran for president 11 years ago. I rejected his effort to blame diversity hiring for the American Airlines 5342 tragedy before investigators had established what happened. That doesn’t make every argument against him a good one.

The Times introduces Fallows as an instrument-rated private pilot with more than 2,000 hours. That’s true but while he’s written extensively about aviation it’s an odd way to frame him as author of this story.

He’s also Jimmy Carter’s former chief speechwriter, a longtime Atlantic writer and editor, and a familiar voice on NPR. His career has been about politics and public policy.

His own introduction to the article frames the piece as meant to connect aviation under Trump with public health, international aid, diplomacy, higher education and the rule of law. Aviation is the vehicle for a larger political point. The piece isn’t really about aviation, it’s using aviation. The Independent NTSB Attacks The Piece The NTSB says the article: Distorts the agency’s responses with political statements intended to attack the current administration. Mischaracterizes the NTSB’s role in the 1,500 hour pilot requirement. Incorrectly implies negligence by the Black Hawk pilots involved in the Washington National Airport collision. The statement also thanks the administration for supporting the agency and providing resources. That last part is political messaging too. (Gratitude for a budget doesn’t answer address aviation safety, but is probably necessary as a defensive measure.) “We are disappointed that this article sensationalizes and politicizes tragedies.” The NTSB’s response to the New York Times Magazine. — NTSB Newsroom (@NTSB_Newsroom) November 12, 2026 Fallows interviewed NTSB Chair Jennifer Homendy. The agency argues she’s being mischaracterized, but they don’t actually say how. In the article she says, “Recommendations alone don’t save lives. Action does.” Her own July statement about the Washington crash warned that delays implementing the board’s recommendations represented a continuing, unacceptable risk. The Article Gets The Colgan Air Crash Wrong Fallows spends considerable space describing the 2009 Colgan Air crash near Buffalo – as the last U.S. airline crash with loss of life before American 5342. In fact, PenAir flight 3296 overran the runway at Unalaska in October 2019, killing a passenger. His account has the Colgan Air plane accumulating ice until it becomes a “frigid brick.” He writes that by the time the captain recognized the problem, it “might already have become unflyable.” But that isn’t what the investigation found. The FAA’s accident analysis says icing wasn’t a cause. When the stall warning activated, the airplane wasn’t yet stalled. The captain pulled back on the controls and put it into a stall. The crew had failed to monitor their airspeed and mishandled the approach. And both Colgan pilots already had more than 1,500 hours: The captain had 3,379 hours. The captain had a history of training failures despite the hours. The first officer had 2,244 hours, including 1,470 hours as a light airplane flight instructor and 774 hours in turbine aircraft. The 1,500 Hour Rule For Co-Pilots Isn’t The Reason For The Safety Improvements After Colgan, Congress and regulators made several changes. Pilot rest rules improved. Training changed. Pilot qualification requirements changed. Sharing pilot records improved. Evidence that safety has performed well after a package of changes doesn’t establish that (1) every element of those changes (2) caused the improvements. And there’s strong reason to believe the the 1,500 hour rule has the opposite effect. It makes becoming a pilot take far longer and become far more costly, which keeps talented people from becoming pilots. Those with a higher opportunity cost, or lacking access to resources needed, get excluded from the pool of potential pilots. You’re then drawing from a smaller group. The NTSB didn’t recommend the 1,500 hour number. In 2010 Senate testimony, then-chair Deborah Hersman explained that the board had investigated accidents involving both high-time and low-time pilots. It found a relationship between failed practical tests and subsequent accidents, but there wasn’t evidence that number of hours actually mattered. The higher minimum took effect in 2013. Fallows’s safety streak begins in February 2009. The first four and a half years of that streak can’t be credited to a requirement that wasn’t yet in effect. Making pilots chase hours can allow relevant skills to deteriorate and bad habits to develop before an airline has to train those habits out of them. Repeated flights in clear weather, making takeoffs and landings at the same familiar airports, aren’t equivalent to structured training for instrument flying in a jet with another pilot, airline procedures, and complex air traffic control instructions. Hours in a hot air balloon count. The hot air balloon can be tethered. European co-pilots don’t face the same restriction, but their safety record is equivalent. Sub-1,500 hour European pilots fly in the United States every day without concern (and American pilots fly in Europe without elevated risk). The argument for the rule is that experience can build judgment, and that the higher threshold was part of a successful improvement in regional airline standards. But that’s an argument for useful experience and rigorous standards. That’s not what we got. An hour spent building time isn’t as valuable as an hour spent in a supervised airline training program. And there’s zero evidence that 1,500 is the right metric. It was chosen because it’s expensive and time-consuming, restricts entry into the pilot profession and drives up wages, and was supported by the major pilots union. The Black Hawk Crew Bears Responsibility The NTSB objected to Fallows implying negligence by the helicopter pilots. But its own probable cause finding identifies the helicopter crew’s failure to maintain effective visual separation as a cause of the collision. The crew accepted responsibility for avoiding the airliner and failed to do so. The existence of systemic failures don’t eliminate pilot error. In Homendy’s testimony explaining the findings, the board concluded that the helicopter’s altimeter probably understated its altitude by about 100 feet. The crew likely believed it was below the route’s ceiling. Fallows also describes the crew flying through civilian traffic without monitoring its traffic avoidance systems. The helicopter had no integrated cockpit traffic display. The pilots had tablets capable of showing traffic, but looking down at them while flying low at night with night vision goggles was problematic. Their audio alerts weren’t integrated into the pilots’ helmets. There were failures of FAA route design, and the FAA failed to act in the face of repeated warnings. But it’s genuinely weird to pin the blame for that on the first nine days of the Trump administration before the crash. I’ve criticized efforts to preserve military exceptions around Washington National. Civilian aviation shouldn’t have to accommodate unnecessary military risks. The Pentagon needs to change, and the current administration is responsible for making that happen. But it didn’t invent the culture that allowed these risks to exist in the first place. The Article’s Own Incident Data Doesn’t Support A Partisan Cause Fallows combines airline accidents, rocket debris, military encounters, airshow incidents and presidential flyovers which generally have different causes. And a list that attributes incidents while a given President is in office to that President doesn’t do a good job of evaluating what decisions caused those incidents. But more simply, even the article’s own figures for the most serious runway incursions are problematic for the Fallows narrative: 2023: 22 incidents. 2024: 7 incidents. 2025: 14 incidents. 2026 through October: 9 incidents. The increase from 2024 to 2025 looks dramatic – a doubling! But it’s still far lower than under Biden in 2023! These are relatively small numbers, and they don’t show a steadily worsening trend. (And there are more aircraft operations than in 2023.) Air Traffic Control Was Broken Before Trump Fallows points to deregulation and falling fares as sources of overcrowding. More people being able to afford to fly is a good thing, and flying is still safer than driving. The failure is an FAA air traffic organization system that hasn’t kept up with demand. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election. Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal. And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. I’ve criticized Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy for counting initial trainee hires as progress, that strikes me as juking the stats but hasn’t been a safety issue. And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place. I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider. And a list that attributes incidents while a given President is in office to that President doesn’t do a good job of evaluating what decisions caused those incidents. But more simply, even the article’s own figures for the most serious runway incursions are problematic for the Fallows narrative: 2023: 22 incidents. 2024: 7 incidents. 2025: 14 incidents. 2026 through October: 9 incidents. The increase from 2024 to 2025 looks dramatic – a doubling! But it’s still far lower than under Biden in 2023! These are relatively small numbers, and they don’t show a steadily worsening trend. (And there are more aircraft operations than in 2023.) Air Traffic Control Was Broken Before Trump Fallows points to deregulation and falling fares as sources of overcrowding. More people being able to afford to fly is a good thing, and flying is still safer than driving. The failure is an FAA air traffic organization system that hasn’t kept up with demand. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election. Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal. And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. I’ve criticized Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy for counting initial trainee hires as progress, that strikes me as juking the stats but hasn’t been a safety issue. And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place. I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider. (HT: Paddle Your Own Kanoo) Topics on this pageAviation safetyDonald TrumpJames FallowsNational Transportation Safety BoardThe New York TimesAir traffic controlAmerican AirlinesBuffaloCanadaColgan AirColgan Air Flight 3407Federal Aviation AdministrationJimmy CarterJoe BidenNational Public RadioRonald Reagan Washington National AirportSean DuffyThe TimesUnited States CongressUnited States Senate+15 more More From View from the Wing

After Colgan, Congress and regulators made several changes. Pilot rest rules improved. Training changed. Pilot qualification requirements changed. Sharing pilot records improved. Evidence that safety has performed well after a package of changes doesn’t establish that (1) every element of those changes (2) caused the improvements.

And there’s strong reason to believe the the 1,500 hour rule has the opposite effect. It makes becoming a pilot take far longer and become far more costly, which keeps talented people from becoming pilots. Those with a higher opportunity cost, or lacking access to resources needed, get excluded from the pool of potential pilots. You’re then drawing from a smaller group.

The NTSB didn’t recommend the 1,500 hour number. In 2010 Senate testimony, then-chair Deborah Hersman explained that the board had investigated accidents involving both high-time and low-time pilots. It found a relationship between failed practical tests and subsequent accidents, but there wasn’t evidence that number of hours actually mattered.

The higher minimum took effect in 2013. Fallows’s safety streak begins in February 2009. The first four and a half years of that streak can’t be credited to a requirement that wasn’t yet in effect.

Making pilots chase hours can allow relevant skills to deteriorate and bad habits to develop before an airline has to train those habits out of them. Repeated flights in clear weather, making takeoffs and landings at the same familiar airports, aren’t equivalent to structured training for instrument flying in a jet with another pilot, airline procedures, and complex air traffic control instructions.

Hours in a hot air balloon count. The hot air balloon can be tethered.

European co-pilots don’t face the same restriction, but their safety record is equivalent. Sub-1,500 hour European pilots fly in the United States every day without concern (and American pilots fly in Europe without elevated risk). The argument for the rule is that experience can build judgment, and that the higher threshold was part of a successful improvement in regional airline standards. But that’s an argument for useful experience and rigorous standards. That’s not what we got. An hour spent building time isn’t as valuable as an hour spent in a supervised airline training program. And there’s zero evidence that 1,500 is the right metric. It was chosen because it’s expensive and time-consuming, restricts entry into the pilot profession and drives up wages, and was supported by the major pilots union. The Black Hawk Crew Bears Responsibility The NTSB objected to Fallows implying negligence by the helicopter pilots. But its own probable cause finding identifies the helicopter crew’s failure to maintain effective visual separation as a cause of the collision. The crew accepted responsibility for avoiding the airliner and failed to do so. The existence of systemic failures don’t eliminate pilot error. In Homendy’s testimony explaining the findings, the board concluded that the helicopter’s altimeter probably understated its altitude by about 100 feet. The crew likely believed it was below the route’s ceiling. Fallows also describes the crew flying through civilian traffic without monitoring its traffic avoidance systems. The helicopter had no integrated cockpit traffic display. The pilots had tablets capable of showing traffic, but looking down at them while flying low at night with night vision goggles was problematic. Their audio alerts weren’t integrated into the pilots’ helmets. There were failures of FAA route design, and the FAA failed to act in the face of repeated warnings. But it’s genuinely weird to pin the blame for that on the first nine days of the Trump administration before the crash. I’ve criticized efforts to preserve military exceptions around Washington National. Civilian aviation shouldn’t have to accommodate unnecessary military risks. The Pentagon needs to change, and the current administration is responsible for making that happen. But it didn’t invent the culture that allowed these risks to exist in the first place. The Article’s Own Incident Data Doesn’t Support A Partisan Cause Fallows combines airline accidents, rocket debris, military encounters, airshow incidents and presidential flyovers which generally have different causes. And a list that attributes incidents while a given President is in office to that President doesn’t do a good job of evaluating what decisions caused those incidents. But more simply, even the article’s own figures for the most serious runway incursions are problematic for the Fallows narrative: 2023: 22 incidents. 2024: 7 incidents. 2025: 14 incidents. 2026 through October: 9 incidents. The increase from 2024 to 2025 looks dramatic – a doubling! But it’s still far lower than under Biden in 2023! These are relatively small numbers, and they don’t show a steadily worsening trend. (And there are more aircraft operations than in 2023.) Air Traffic Control Was Broken Before Trump Fallows points to deregulation and falling fares as sources of overcrowding. More people being able to afford to fly is a good thing, and flying is still safer than driving. The failure is an FAA air traffic organization system that hasn’t kept up with demand. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election. Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal. And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place. I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider. Italy fans mock Domenech for classless tweet during France defeat at San Siro

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Former France national team head coach Raymond Domenech was widely mocked by Italy fans on social media after posting a classless tweet during Thursday evening’s Nations League match between the two countries in San Siro: ‘You really never understood football’.

Italy claimed their first win at home against France in 64 years with a 3-1 victory in San Siro on Thursday evening: Michael Olise, Pio Esposito (x2) and Davide Frattesi with the goals. #

This result saw the Azzurri leapfrog Les Bleus into first place in their Nations League group heading into the final round of group stage fixtures on Sunday night. Roberto Mancini and his players only require a draw against Belgium to qualify for the Nations League quarter-finals.

The media response in Italy has been overwhelmingly positive, Tuttosport claiming that the Azzurri ‘are back’ and the Corriere dello Sport thanking Mancini on the front page of Friday morning’s paper.

The reaction in France, however, has understandably been less positive. Former head coach Domenech did not enjoy what he saw on the night, but opened himself up for criticism after criticising Italy’s performance on the night.

Italie France 1/1 c’est inespéré pour les Italiens . Quelle faiblesse dans tous les secteurs mais le plus surprenant est leur niveau technique , que d’erreurs dans des passes faciles c’est affolant .

— Raymond Domenech (@RaymondDomenech) November 12, 2026

Shortly after Esposito had levelled the scores at 1-1, Domenech tweeted: “Italy 1-1 France, it’s beyond the Italians’ wildest dreams. So weak in every department, but what’s most surprising is their technical level. So many mistakes from easy passes, it’s alarming.”

Frattesi would go on to score for 2-1 in the second half and Esposito added a third just over 10 minutes later, not forgetting that his brother Sebastiano had also missed a penalty.

Domenech has since been treated to a round of mocking responses from Italy supporters on social media.

The most common response to Domenech’s tweet was a video or gif of Fabio Cannavaro lifting the 2006 World Cup trophy, after beating Domenech’s France side in the final in Berlin.

One fan wrote: “You always provide, dear Raymond, thank you for the entertainment.”

“Keep reading your horoscopes, Raymond,” another added.

“You’re as bad an expert as you were a coach,” another taunted. “You really nevery understood football,” wrote another.

Peter Young is a Senior Reporter with Football Italia.

U.S. seizes $1.2 billion in cryptocurrency stolen from Binance

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

U.S. authorities have seized around $1.2 billion in virtual assets stolen from cryptocurrency exchange Binance last month, the Department of Justice said Thursday, as it vowed action to tackle cyberscams originating from North Korea.

Over $6 billion was stolen from industry giant Binance on Oct. 29, which the DOJ attributed to actors linked to the North Korean military.

Collective efforts between the U.S. government and private sector have resulted in more than $2.7 billion of that amount being frozen.

The FBI blames the attack, which shook confidence in crypto market security, on members of the North Korean military it refers to as "TraderTraitor."

The DOJ said it was taking coordinated action against what it called the North Korean government's "schemes to fund its regime through the massive theft of cryptocurrency."

That includes "the court-authorized seizure and restraint of more than $1.2 billion worth of cryptocurrency recently stolen from Binance," it said.

DOJ units, law enforcement partners and virtual asset service providers have been working to intercept the stolen assets.

But efforts to track and freeze the assets are ongoing, as TraderTraitor actors continue to launder them through various cryptocurrency channels.

Authorities have also seized cloud-based infrastructure used in a massive identity impersonation scam known as "FaceStealer," which has seen more than $400 million worth of cryptocurrency stolen over 18 months, the DOJ said.

The scheme sees hackers set up fake video meetings using stolen accounts and recordings of people known to the target by using AI-generated deepfakes. The hackers then say a technical problem requires a fix, install malware on a computer and take over the victim's communications, steal their cryptocurrency and target their contacts — sometimes months or more than a year after the initial compromise.

Individuals working in the cryptocurrency and web3 industries are prime targets.

Assistant Director in Charge Patrick Grandy, of the FBI's Los Angeles Field Office, called FaceStealer "a highly effective social engineering scheme capable of deceiving even the most experienced technical and security conscious computer user."

"It exploits our trust in ubiquitous video conference platforms and valued personal relationships. If you can't imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough," Grandy added.

"North Korea's government bankrolls its rogue regime and illicit weapons program through cryptocurrency theft," Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General for National Security M. Scott Proctor said in a statement.

"America will fight back against that corrupt regime's effort to profit through cybercrime. The National Security Division will use every tool at its disposal to seize and return North Korea's ill-gotten gains."

‘Ramayana,’ ‘Fatherland,’ ‘Dhurandhar’ Lead International Film Festival of India Lineup Featuring Almodovar, Kore-eda, Kurosawa

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Paweł Pawlikowski’s “Fatherland,” which earned him the best director prize at Cannes this year, will open the 57th International Film Festival of India (IFFI), set for Nov. 20-28 in Goa.

Nitesh Tiwari’s “Ramayana: Rise of a Legend” will open the festival’s BRICS showcase, and Aditya Dhar’s Bollywood feature “Dhurandhar” will open the Indian Panorama.

Goa chief minister Pramod Sawant unveiled the program at a curtain-raiser press conference in the state, joined by festival director Ashutosh Gowariker, the filmmaker behind “Lagaan.” Organizers received more than 1,900 submissions from 137 countries for this edition, and the lineup spans more than 150 international films from 86 countries. Related Stories Gkids Acquires U.S. Rights to Cartoon Saloon’s Animated Film 'Julián,' Directed by Louise Bagnall and Executive Produced by Zoe Saldaña (EXCLUSIVE) Arnold Schwarzenegger Explains to Jimmy Kimmel Why He’s ‘Absolutely’ Still a Republican After the Blue ‘Tsunami’ Midterms

“Fatherland” is set in 1949. It tracks Nobel laureate Thomas Mann and his daughter Erika as they travel through a Germany split by the aftermath of war. The Poland, Germany, Italy and France co-production plays in German, English, French and Russian.

Alina Șerban’s “I Matter,” a Romania, Germany and Belgium co-production in Romanian and Romani, will screen as the mid-fest film. Anthony Chen’s Singaporean comedy-drama “We Are All Strangers,” in Mandarin and English, will close the festival.

Chanchal Kumar, secretary of India’s Ministry of Information & Broadcasting, quoted Prime Minister Narendra Modi in discussing this year’s theme, “Stories Without Borders”: “The stories may have an address, but emotions have no borders.”

The BRICS section will feature work from all 11 member nations of the bloc as well as its partner countries. Titles include Jessica Beshir’s “Faya Dayi” and Anastasia Kuzina’s “Bo Bo.”

Spain will bring four films as country of honor: Pedro Almodóvar’s “Bitter Christmas,” Jaime Lorente’s “The Bad Son,” Laura Alvea and José Ortuño’s “Trinidad” and Juana Macías’ “Looking for Michael.” Russia’s partner-country showcase pairs Kuzina’s “Bo Bo” with Nikita Grushin’s “New Home,” Raul Geidarov’s “Sidney Lumet’s Birthday” and Gerbert Rappaport’s musical “Cheryomushki.”

The festival will screen 76 Indian films across 18 languages. Indian Panorama selectors weighed 404 feature and 358 non-feature submissions, while the best web series (OTT) category attracted 46 eligible entries. Leena Yadav’s Ladakhi-language “Becoming” will open the non-feature strand.

Madhur Bhandarkar chairs the feature film jury and Himanshu Shekhar Khatua heads the non-feature film jury.

Indian director-producer Aanand L. Rai (“Raanjhanaa,” “Tanu Weds Manu”) will chair the international competition jury. He will be joined by Argentine filmmaker Sol Berruezo Pichon-Riviere; Thai writer-director Ratchapoom Boonbunchachoke, whose “A Useful Ghost” won the grand prize at Cannes Critics’ Week in 2025; Colombian sound designer María Alejandra Rojas; and Romanian writer-director Bogdan Mureșanu (“The New Year That Never Came”).

The Cinema of the World section includes Kore-eda Hirokazu’s “Look Back,” Kurosawa Kiyoshi’s “The Samurai and the Prisoner,” Lukas Dhont’s “Coward,” Nishikawa Miwa’s “Children Untold,” Gábor Reisz’s “Ground Floor” and Wim Wenders’ “From Inside Out – The Architecture of Peter Zumthor.” From Festivals Worldwide gathers Hong Sang-soo’s “Nowhere to Lay My Eyes,” Emin Alper’s “Salvation,” Valeska Grisebach’s “The Dreamed Adventure” and Grant Gee’s “Everybody Digs Bill Evans.”

Two sections are new this year, with five films each. Animation includes Brad Bird’s “Ray Gunn.” Films About Films was created after leading filmmakers suggested it. Its titles include Barnaby Thompson’s “Maverick: The Epic Adventures of David Lean” and Francesco Zippel’s “Vittorio De Sica – Staging Life.”

Seven first features will compete for the best debut feature prize: “Fading Man” (Germany), “The Sledge” (India), “Paris” (Belgium), “The Black Sea” (Egypt), “L’Âge d’Or (The Golden Age)” (France and Italy), “Birds on Ice” (India) and “Atlantic” (China). The wider program counts 52 women directors and 62 first-time filmmakers.

Restored Classics will screen Don Siegel’s “Invasion of the Body Snatchers,” Danny Boyle’s “Trainspotting,” David Lynch’s “Wild at Heart,” F.W. Murnau’s “Sunrise: A Song of Two Humans” and Ernst Lubitsch’s “To Be or Not to Be.” Andrzej Wajda gets a five-film centenary tribute. Indian figures born a century ago will also be honored: directors Shakti Samanta and Raj Khosla, actors Uttam Kumar, Suryakant Mandhare, Prem Nazir and Raj Kumar, and composers O.P. Nayyar and Ravi.

The WAVES Film Bazaar market will host 150 invited guests, including buyers and production companies. Its program covers a Screenwriters’ Lab, a Co-production Market, a Viewing Room and a Work-in-Progress Lab. Netflix is the bazaar’s Knowledge Series partner and will present the gala premiere of “Pushpak Viman,” alongside an international screening of “Ray Gunn.” The streamer will also highlight its work with the Indian government on building the country’s VFX talent base.

Screenings will take place on 13 indoor screens at four locations: Panaji, Porvorim, Margao and Ponda. Together they seat more than 4,000.

IFFI was launched in 1952 and ranks among Asia’s oldest film festivals. It is organized by the information and broadcasting ministry with the Goa government and the Entertainment Society of Goa.

I hate how most smartwatches look, but I'll happily wear the Withings ScanWatch Light every day and with any outfit

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock and manage exclusive Tom’s Guide member rewards.

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

If you’re after a beautiful smartwatch with all the basic fitness and health features you need, and which won’t ruin your outfit, look no further than the Withings ScanWatch Light 2. This elegant watch is pricey given its basic functionality, but still offers strong battery life and lots of insight via the companion app. If it’s bang for your buck you’re after, rivals offer more, but none can match the ScanWatch Light’s sheer pizzazz.

Auto activity detection didn’t work for me

Why you can trust Tom's Guide Our writers and editors spend hours analyzing and reviewing products, services, and apps to help find what's best for you. Find out more about how we test, analyze, and rate.

I hate most fitness trackers. Although I love what they do, I really don’t love how they look. For ages now, I’ve been pining for a smartwatch that does all the stuff I need it to when I’m at the gym or on a walk, yet looks like a normal, classic watch. I know, I know, you can set analog watch faces on many smartwatches, but we all know that’s not the same.

Enter the Withings ScanWatch Light. This thing is beautiful. It looks like a classic watch and fits in with even my smartest outfits. And it does all the basic fitness-y things I need it to, although not much more — the “Light” part really does mean light. This is not a feature-packed smartwatch, and certainly isn’t designed for athletes. Instead, it’s a smartwatch for people who want core health features without sacrificing style or ruining the cohesiveness of their outfits.

Find out whether this is the best fitness tracker for you in my full Withings ScanWatch Light review.

White/gray; rose gold/white; black/silver

The Withings ScanWatch Light costs $249 / £229 at Amazon. If you buy direct from Withings, you can choose a metal or leather strap for an extra $49, or a fabric strap for $39.

It certainly isn’t cheap, but look: Withings gear is always pricey. I don’t mind something being expensive if it can back up the price tag somehow. And most Withings products do so by combining charming design, solid build quality, and decent core functionality.

On the first two counts, the ScanWatch Light ticks the boxes. However, this is a stripped-back watch, so price versus functionality is pretty poor. You’re going to get more features for the money with the $249 Apple Watch SE 3. Or the $169 Amazfit Active Premium 3. Or heck, even the $79 Amazfit Bip 6. None match the ScanWatch Light’s elegant looks, though, so my question is: how much is style worth to you?

If you have the extra cash and want similar styling but more features, check out the ScanWatch Light’s bigger siblings: the $369 Withings ScanWatch 2 or $599 ScanWatch Nova.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

In my opinion, the best thing about the ScanWatch Light is its design. I really appreciate the huge array of fitness features of smartwatches like my Amazfit T-Rex 3 Pro. But I much prefer wearing a traditional analog watch when I’m not working out — giant AMOLED slabs and ugly silicone straps don’t really go well with my daily outfits. So, up to now I’ve switched between my analog watch and smartwatch when going to/from the gym, which has been a pain.

The ScanWatch Light is the perfect solution. It looks just like a classic analog watch: simple, elegant, beautiful. The face measures 1.4 inches, which fits my wrist nicely without feeling too bulky.

The watch comes in three different colorways (white/gray, rose gold/white, and black/silver). I chose the black model and love its effortless style and sophistication. It pairs well with all my outfits, from casual daily and smart casual fits up to suits and even my black tux. And the silver bezel and accents pair nicely with the silver and white gold rings I wear on my left hand. Obviously, though, the ScanWatch Light is also a fitness tracker! Which means I have a single watch for both gym, work, and leisure. I never have to switch again.

The standard strap is fluoroelastomer (a type of rubber), so it doesn’t absorb sweat or go gross when working out, and doesn’t chafe either. That said, while I usually dislike the look of rubber/silicone, the ScanWatch Light’s strap is finished with a matte effect, meaning it doesn’t look tacky like my (much cheaper) Amazfit Active. Again, you’re getting the best of both worlds.

I found the strap either a little snug or slightly too loose. In other words, I’m between sizes. This depends entirely on the individual anatomy/width of your wrist, though, so I’m not moaning. And besides, it still wasn’t uncomfortable at all, either when exercising or wearing all day — helped by the ScanWatch Light’s low 0.9-ounce kerb weight.

The buckle is dual-sided, meaning it also serves as a fixed loop to hold the strap tail in place, and there’s a sliding free loop for a second securement point. It’s a solid system and keeps the strap tail from flapping around. It can be a bit fiddly getting the strap through the second buckle opening, though.

As I’d expect from a Withings product, the ScanWatch Light is built extremely well and feels premium. The case is made from stainless steel and the face is covered by Gorilla Glass (the kind used on smartphone screens) so should stand up to daily wear and tear without scratching. It’s waterproof down to 5ATM (five atmospheres) of pressure, so you can take it swimming.

The digital grayscale OLED display is very small, but deliberately so — this is designed to be as minimalist a smartwatch as possible. When you press the digital crown, the watch hands move out of the way of the screen automatically if they need to, which I love. When it’s not in use, the display stays off and thereby blends in nicely with the black watchface, thus not spoiling the analog aesthetic. On the lighter models, though, the black display is always visible.

You control the watch entirely via the steel digital crown. Navigating the menu is fairly clunky, especially when you want to go back through menus. However, the menu system and features aren’t complex to begin with, so it’s fine. You don’t need to use the crown to set the time or date, as it’s all done digitally by pairing the watch to your phone and the Withings app.

As I mentioned up top, this watch bears the “Light” moniker for a reason. If you’re after highly detailed tracking and plenty of on-wrist control/info, the ScanWatch Light isn’t for you.

Don’t get me wrong, there’s a decent array of activities on offer. All the basics are there, of course: running, cycling, rowing, etc. There are indoor versions for treadmills and exercise bikes, as well as some specific activities like badminton, muay thai and more. There’s even a dog walking activity — I’m not sure how much that differs from ordinary walking.

None of the activity modes I used were particularly in-depth, though. Weight lifting, for example, had no interval functionality. They all seem to just measure BPM as you work out. Still, that’s all I really need. (And to be honest, I find the interval weight lifting function on my Amazfit watches a bit fiddly anyway.) You have no real control over the activities on-watch either — you simply use the digital crown to pause and then either resume or stop activities. Nor do you get any detailed info on the watch itself as to your performance or progress.

If you’re a serious fitness enthusiast and want something for hardcore training, you’ll want more in-depth measurements and onboard controls. Check out the Apple Watch Series 12, Withings ScanWatch 2 or Amazfit Active Premium 3. Or the Apple Watch Ultra 3 if budget is no constraint.

What the ScanWatch Light measured was pretty accurate. I compared the BPM analysis and calorific burn to several different machines at my gym and got essentially the same results each time, usually within just a couple of BPM and a couple of dozen calories. The ScanWatch did tell me I had mild bradycardia at first, measuring 52BPM resting. Look, I’m not in terrible shape, but that’s athlete levels, and I was highly stressed at the time due to a bereavement. So go figure. Since then, though, BPM reads have stayed more realistic and consistent. There’s also a VO2 max assessment available via the app — arguably the most important metric to track, generally speaking.

Unfortunately, the ScanWatch Light’s automatic activity detection simply doesn’t work on my test sample. Anything I did at the gym just went completely undetected. I cycled hard for 8 minutes. I ran for 10 minutes. Nothing, until I started the activities manually. I thought it might use GPS movement to detect activities, but when I walked or jogged outside, nothing happened. Weirder yet, on two occasions I've woken up in the night to find the watch has started detecting a run while on my bedside table.

I’ve reached out to Withings to find out why this may be and whether it’s an isolated incident on my test sample. This isn’t a gamechanger for me — I often find automatic detection results in false starts on my Amazfit Active and T-Rex 3 Pro, so I typically disable it anyway. But if a product has a feature, I still expect it to work.

The ScanWatch Light doesn’t have built-in GPS, instead relying on your phone for location services. This means you’ll need to keep your phone on you when you exercise if you want to view routes.

Health tracking-wise, it’s a similar story. The ScanWatch Light doesn’t track much. It’ll measure your BPM as I mentioned above, but there’s no ECG, blood oxygen, or body temperature tracking — you’ll have to upgrade to the pricier ScanWatch 2 or ScanWatch Nova for those.

Aside from BPM, the ScanWatch Light can track your sleep quality and give you an assessment of your breathing quality during sleep — both found on the pricier ScanWatch 2. There’s also menstrual cycle tracking. It’s more of an app feature, but you can add cycle tracking to the watch menu to input your stages. As a dude, I obviously didn’t use this, but it’s still a fundamental feature I expect to see in a fitness tracker.

There’s also a relaxation mode, which is pretty cool. You set a time interval, then how many breaths per minute you want to take. The watch then vibrates to prompt your breaths in and out. It’s a cool little feature for quickly taking a wellbeing break. And there are standing prompts when the watch detects you’ve not stood for a while — all pretty par for the course.

The Withings app is usually one of our favorites. It allows you to customize the ScanWatch Light’s menus and offers lots of information and data based on your measurements, inputs and watch readings. You can track your activities, progress and health data, with lots of graphs for visualization. The app provides a strong basic level of insight for all of this, explaining what terms mean and often linking out to informative articles for detailed explanation. As you can see in the screenshot below, I've tapped through from my BPM graph, to a basic explanation and second chart, and again through to a full detailed heart rate explainer article.

Via the Withings app, all your health data can be synced from centralized apps, like Apple Health, Google Health, or Samsung Health. Withings also partners with major tracking apps like Strava and MyFitnessPal, although if you’re using an iPhone like me, all this is routed through Apple Health to begin with anyway, so I didn’t need to set up any specific connections.

After one week with constant use and workouts 4-5 times per week, I still had 33% of battery left. So you can reasonably expect around 10 days with normal usage. A full charge takes around 2 hours on average, and charging via a 15W charger from 30% took me around an hour and 15 minutes, which tracks with Withings’ claims.

I’m not a fan of the ScanWatch Light’s charging dock. It’s just an awkwardly long piece of thin, bent plastic with an ostensibly meaningless lip on one side. It doesn’t sit particularly well on my bedside table, looks ugly, and struggles to stay in position. Great.

I much prefer the small, unobtrusive dock of my T-Rex 3 Pro, and Apple’s sleek little magnetic dock. But in fairness to Withings, I resent using all docks equally as much — they’re just a sad reality given that sticking a USB-C port on a watch would seriously compromise pressure- and waterproofing.

Look, the Withings ScanWatch Light is overpriced and under-featured for the money. I can admit that. It’s not for athletes or serious fitness enthusiasts. But it isn’t meant for those people. It’s for people like me who want a stylish all-round watch that can track all the basics for general day-to-day fitness and wellbeing without getting too serious. And importantly, for people who don’t want an ugly square hunk of AMOLED display on their wrist, clashing with their outfit.

It’s also built beautifully, has solid battery life and offers lots of visualizations and information via the Withings app. And did I mention it looks fantastic? I know the ScanWatch Light isn’t perfect, but damn, do I love it.

Peter is a Senior Editor at Tom's Guide, heading up the site's Reviews team and Cameras section. As a writer, he covers topics including tech, photography, gaming, hardware, motoring and food & drink. Outside of work, he's an avid photographer, specialising in architectural and portrait photography. When he's not snapping away on his beloved Fujifilm camera, he can usually be found telling everyone about his greyhounds, riding his motorcycle, squeezing as many FPS as possible out of PC games, and perfecting his espresso shots.
