How to Lose Weight Naturally, According to Experts

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Here are 10 easy-to-follow tips to speed up the process—and make it more enjoyable.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Starting a weight-loss journey is no easy feat. There’s a lot to think about, between getting into a regular workout routine, changing up what you eat, and maybe even considering a GLP-1 medication.

But at its core, weight loss is about developing a healthy, sustainable relationship with food and movement. You don’t have to make a dozen changes all at once—the goal is long-term success, even if it’s a slow process. That’s why you should aim to lose one to two pounds a week, says Danielle Crumble Smith, RDN, a dietitian at Eat Well, Live Well. Working out at a manageable pace, eating balanced meals, and making maintainable changes can help you feel your best.

Meet the experts: Danielle Crumble Smith, RDN, is a nutritionist at Eat Well, Live Well. Kami Blease, CPT, is a personal trainer at Fyt Personal Training. Tatyana Johnston, CPT, is a former Nike master trainer and event specialist at Lululemon. Jordan Hill, RD, is a nutritionist with Top Nutrition Coaching.

Ahead, experts share their 10 best tips to make your weight-loss journey successful.

First, be sure to consult your doctor—they’ll help you set mindful, attainable goals that will keep your body healthy and strong. Then, try implementing more of these habits into your lifestyle:

Take the stairs every day at work or hold a quick plank first thing in the morning. "All these things add up over time," says Kami Blease, CPT, a personal trainer at Fyt Personal Training. "I recommend finding things you could do almost daily.”

Additionally, try adding a brisk 30-minute walk into your day (even if it’s broken up into three 10-minute walks) to increase calorie burn and push you closer to your weight-loss goals, per the Mayo Clinic. "It's low impact and not a super strenuous form of movement, which increases the chances that someone will stick with it,” says Tatyana Johnston, CPT, a former Nike master trainer and event specialist at Lululemon. "Just remember to recover with hydration, fuel, and stretching.”

Burn fat and build muscle with the WH+ 4-week walking plan

Strength training “helps with balance and stability, improves metabolism, and even lowers your risk of certain diseases," says Blease. Resistance training has also been found to help with weight loss and body recomposition, according to a 2026 study in Frontiers in Endocrinology that had 304 adults follow a calorie-restricted diet and either resistance train, do aerobic exercise, or no exercise for 1 to 18 months.

To get comfortable with form, try practicing bodyweight exercises first, Blease recommends, then add weights after you’ve mastered the move. This can help you focus on progressive overload—which means gradually increasing the weight, frequency, or number of reps over time, Blease says. Eventually, you should aim to work all major muscle groups at least twice a week, per the new ACSM resistance training guidelines.

Cardio can also help with weight loss—the CDC recommends 150 minutes of moderate-intensity aerobic activity (such as brisk walking) per week, or 75 minutes of vigorous-intensity activity (like running) each week. However, this number can vary based on someone’s individual health, and the intensity of the cardio. "Consistency is key. If you can establish and stick to a cardio schedule, you have a better chance at losing weight and keeping it off," Johnston says. Playing sports, working out with friends, or listening to a podcast during cardio can help boost your motivation too.

Also, if you enjoy high-impact exercises and want to lose weight fast, HIIT workouts can be a great way to expend a lot of energy in a short period of time. "You can ramp up the intensity by trying to get in more reps during a certain amount of time or using heavier weights while maintaining the same time goal,” says Blease. “Both will increase your intensity and continue to challenge your body."

If the gym or workout classes aren’t really your speed, choose a form of movement that you love so you can stay consistent with it. “Hiking, swimming, skiing, surfing, or biking are all forms of movement that might feel more sustainable,” says Blease.

"Start smaller than you might think," Blease says. "Often, people overcommit because they are excited. However, it's then easy to get burnt out." After a few weeks, add in a second day, and when that feels seamless, try a third, and over time, you'll find a cadence that works for you, she adds.

Just make sure you always have at least one rest day. "People can get so fixated on weight loss that they end up [overdoing exercise and] hurting themselves," Blease says. "Take time to stretch, rest, and recover as needed."

"Having a trainer create a program that is specific to your needs ensures that you are more likely to get the results you are looking for—and keep your body safe in the process," says Blease. They can help you lose fat while building muscle, which can better your overall metabolic health and insulin sensitivity, and regulate blood sugar to support a healthier body recomposition.

A trainer will also make sure you maintain proper form and that you stay consistent. Decide on a number of days per week that fits your goal and budget, and ask your trainer to help hold you accountable, Blease says.

Research suggests that working with a personal trainer may have more profound effects on weight loss: A 2024 study in Heliyon had 66 adults train individually, with a partner, or a personal trainer over 12 weeks, and they monitored their improvements in bench presses, squats, skeletal muscle mass, and fat mass. The group that worked with a personal trainer improved their leg training and reduced fat mass compared to the other groups.

Protein can help you stay full throughout the day, stabilize your blood sugar levels, and synthesize muscle mass, Smith says, helping with weight loss. While individual dietary needs vary, you can generally aim for 30 to 35 grams of protein per meal to help you stay full throughout the day. Some good sources include eggs, poultry, seafood, red meat, beans, peas, lentils, legumes, nuts, seeds, and soy, per the current Dietary Guidelines for Americans.

Additionally, "fiber is more slowly digested and helps keep you feeling full for a longer period,” Smith adds, recommending vegetables. Increased vegetable intake is also linked to lower blood sugar levels, lower triglycerides, and can help promote liver function. If you don't love veggies, you can also puree them and add them to a sauce or weight-loss-friendly smoothie.

Lastly, don’t forget about healthy (unsaturated) fats—which can help provide energy, per the Mayo Clinic. Try avocados, extra-virgin olive oil, nuts, seeds, soybeans, and seafood.

"Drinking enough water can help you to feel full and prevent overeating," says dietitian Jordan Hill, RD. Aim to drink about half your body weight in ounces of hydrating fluids per day.

Also, avoid sugary beverages. "Juices, sodas, and fancy coffees are often the calorie-equivalent of a meal, yet they will still leave you hungry," says Smith. "The sugar content is often more than a candy bar, which will cause blood sugar levels to rise." Instead, replace your beverages with fruit-infused water, herbal tea, or coffee with cinnamon or zero-calorie sweeteners.

Avoid foods that are high in added sugars, unhealthy fats, and empty calories, which can contribute to weight gain, says Hill: "Instead, focus on eating whole, nutrient-dense foods that will provide your body with the nutrients it needs."

Listening to your internal cues of hunger and fullness can help you control portion sizes, which can also aid weight loss. "Honoring fullness can help prevent overeating and ensure that you're not consuming more calories than your body needs," says Hill. Keeping a food journal can also help you become more mindful of what you're eating, when, and how you're feeling throughout the process of losing weight.

“When you skip meals or go too long in between meals or snacks, you get overly hungry. This makes it much more difficult to make healthy choices, and it also makes it harder to not overeat later," says Smith. Skipping meals can also result in a slower metabolism and leave you feeling fatigued, which can negatively impact your workouts both in effort and recovery.

If you struggle to eat consistent meals, Smith recommends munching on small snacks throughout the day. “Set yourself up for success by bringing healthy snacks with you when you’re away from home, so you’re not caught in a situation where you are really hungry but have no access to healthy food,” she says.

If you need extra assistance, a dietitian can analyze your diet and recommend personalized tips for how you can eat to lose weight, which may be more helpful than doing it on your own. A 2025 review of articles on weight-loss interventions provided by a dietitian for overweight and obese adults in the Journal of the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics looked at how working with a dietitian improved their health markers, as opposed to not working with one (the control group). The group that worked with a dietitian had improved weight loss, waist circumference, blood pressure, and quality of life compared to the control group.

When you encounter a setback (like when you skip a few days of workouts), it can be discouraging and all-consuming, and can cause you to fall off track. "One of the most crucial components of successfully reaching a goal is to not let setbacks ruin our progress,” says Johnston. “The setback is often not as severe as we think, and will not impact our end goal very much at all.”

Just take a breath and realize we’re all human and mistakes are normal; then move forward with your health plan in mind.

And don’t forget to be kind to yourself. Practicing self-compassion and returning to your plan will help reinforce your goal and remind you that this weight-loss journey requires patience and perseverance. "Practice giving yourself grace when [you] slip up,” Johnston says. “As long as it doesn’t become a habit, you will continue moving forward towards your goal.”

These smart home lights have stood the test of time

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Over the nine years since I've owned my home, I've had hundreds of smart home devices come in and out of my house. However, there's been one device that's been here nearly the entire time, and has held up pretty well for more than half a decade.

Since I reviewed them in 2020, the Ring Solar Pathlights have been faithfully keeping my front walkway illuminated through blazing summers, rainy springs, and multiple blizzards, where they were covered in more than two feet of snow.

And right now, you can get them on sale in an early Black Friday deal for around 30% off. which makes it a good time to pick them up if you want smart lighting for your home.

The Ring Solar Pathlights do a great job illuminating your walk, and they can be linked with other smart home devices, such as home security cameras and video doorbells.

Among the best solar lights, Ring's lights are handy in that you can link them to other smart home devices. For instance, if a Ring security camera or doorbell detects motion, you can program the lights to turn on automatically.

Conversely, the lights also have their own motion sensors, which can be used to trigger other smart home devices. I don't have anything so complex, but I have the lights set so that they turn on to a low setting in the evening, but then brighten if they detect motion.

Right now, you can find a few different deals on Ring's solar-powered lights:

Ring also sells battery versions of its Pathlights which are slightly cheaper, but I would advise going for the solar-powered models; you don't want to bother with having to recharge the lights yourself, and in the six years I've had the lights, I've never had an issue with them running out of juice.

The one caveat is that the batteries in two of the lights have died, as it looks like water somehow seeped in, and Ring doesn't sell replacement batteries. There are a few threads on Reddit and Ring's site about this, and owners have found that the Nitecore NL1823 18650 battery ($9 each) works as a suitable replacement.

Jamie Dornan & Anthony Mackie Share Hilarious Lesson From Fatherhood

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Jamie Dornan and Anthony Mackie—who are costarring in the thriller series 12 12 12—exclusively told E! News about their kids' reactions to growing up with movie star dads.

Jamie Dornan and Anthony Mackie's most important critics are their kids.

The actors—who first starred in 2019's Synchronic before reuniting for upcoming TV series 12 12 12—confessed that amid their many roles as action stars over the years, earning cool points from their children has been no easy feat.

"If they don't think I'm cool after Lord of the Rings, I'm done," Dornan, who plays Aragorn in the upcoming prequel, The Hunt for Gollum, joked in an exclusive interview with E! News’ Will Marfuggi. "If that's not good enough for my kids, then I don't know what else to offer them."

The 50 Shades of Grey alum brought his three kids Dulcie, 13, Elva, 10 and Alberta, 7, with wife Amelia Warner to stay with him in New Zealand during the fantasy shoot, where they were thankfully impressed by his performance on set.

"Usually, they ask to leave. Like, they come to set, and after half an hour, they're like, 'You're just saying the same thing over and over. This is so boring,'" Dornan admitted. "They came to set one night when I was fighting and beheading orcs and stuff, and they were like, 'It was cool.'"

That said, the 44-year-old was pleased that his little ones found it "good to see Daddy all heroic like that" because, as he put it, "that'll be as cool as it gets for me."

Mackie's brood, on the other hand—which includes four sons with ex-wife Sheletta Chapital—weren't so easily won over by their dad moonlighting as a soldier with supernatural powers, but for good reason. The Captain America actor is already a superhero at home.

"My boys could care less," Mackie remarked with a laugh. "At the end of the day, I realized they just want me to be dad."

And the 48-year-old understands why his family unit is more content with quality time at home, including playing pickleball.

"I love the fact that all they want from me is me," he continued. "But then when I come home, we get to be one big family."

Still, Mackie admitted bringing the crew as his plus-fours to a red carpet was more than a typical 'bring your kids to work' day, even for them.

"They went to the premiere of my movie," Mackie recounted. "They had never seen anything of that scale, like of that magnitude."

And Mackie and Dornan's latest project is yet another grand production. The Marvel alum credited the art department when remarking, "The scale of the show, I feel, is kind of like another character."

The Belfast native agreed the ambitious approach paid off for the series, premiering Nov. 13 on Apple TV, about a meticulously planned fictional robbery of a bank in Zurich.

"It's like the biggest heist in European history," Dornan said, using the British slang for cheap when adding, "It couldn't be pony. It needed to be big."

New episodes drop on Fridays through Dec. 25.

Guide: Here's How To Claim All The Xbox 25th Anniversary In-Game Items

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

That translucent green do be looking fire

Xbox's 25th Anniversary translucent green look is killer, and I'm a big fan of the cosmetics that Xbox's first-party titles have whipped up to support the milestone.

We thought we'd do a quick guide on how to get all Xbox Anniversary items across Forza Horizon 6, Halo: Campaign Evolved, Gears of War: E-Day, Minecraft, Microsoft Flight Simulator 2024, and Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4.

Most of these are free to claim through in-game stores or redemption codes, but it's worth saying the Modern Warfare 4 M4 skin - arguably the best anniversary cosmetic of the lot - will require some sort of spending.

So, here's a look at all the Xbox 25th Anniversary in-game items and how to claim to them:

On this page: Here's How To Claim All The Xbox 25th Anniversary In-Game Items

How to claim: Find it in the Gifts tab of the Message Center

We are celebrating 25 years of XBOX the Horizon way with a new livery for Forza Horizon 6‘s cover car, the 2025 GR GT. This new livery features a unique design that calls back to the original XBOX’s colors and materials. Find it in the Gifts tab of the Message Center!

How to claim: Go to Halo Waypoint's Redemption link and enter this code - GWT9-DNBT-7X9S-DJCX-8KH3

Embrace your inner “X factor” with the Damascus Prototype Armor Style, available to all Halo: Campaign Evolved players. Click this redemption link to unlock it on Halo Waypoint with code GWT9-DNBT-7X9S-DJCX-8KH3

How to claim: Go to the in-game Store and redeem the Xbox Anniversary Weapon Skin Set

To celebrate 25 years of XBOX, players will be able to claim the XBOX Anniversary Weapon Skin Set for free in the Gears of War: E-Day in-game shop. Once our shop rotates the following Tuesday, November 17, the weapon will enter the player barracks where it can be acquired with our free-to-earn grind currency (Coins) or purchased with premium currency.

How to claim: You can claim the creator item from the Minecraft dressing room

Celebrate 25 years of XBOX in blocky style by claiming your free OG Green Character Creator item! This base player skin covers you from head to toe in the iconic XBOX green. You can wear whatever you want over it or keep it as is, the green translucent glow will let you shine either way! Claim it for free in the Minecraft dressing room between November 10 at 10 AM PT and December 10 at 10 AM PT.

*Requires Minecraft: Bedrock Edition (sold separately). 1 Character Creator item per person/account, must be redeemed by December 10, 2026 at 10 AM PT.

How to claim: Automatically added to Microsoft Flight Simulator 2024 owners

Microsoft Flight Simulator pilots, get ready to celebrate a major milestone in gaming history. To mark the 25th Anniversary of XBOX, we’re excited to announce the release of four brand-new commemorative liveries that all throw back to the XBOX original logo and designs. Available automatically to all Microsoft Flight Simulator 2024 owners, these commemorative liveries are available in the Aircraft Livery menu for the Boeing 737 MAX, XCub, Hot Air Balloon, and Skyship 600.

How to claim: By purchasing Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4 Vault Edition, or through the Vault Edition Upgrade on Xbox Series consoles

Players who pre-ordered the game digitally on XBOX Series X|S will view the Inner Circuit Blueprint in a translucent OG Green visual treatment. Designed as a tribute to 25 years of XBOX, the Inner Circuit Blueprint takes cues from classic translucent Xbox hardware. Its transparent green finish exposes the weapon’s internal systems, turning the technology beneath the surface into part of the visual identity.

The 35+ Best Gifts for Moms, According to My Mother’s Mini Me (Yours Truly)

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you purchase an independently reviewed product or service through a link on our website, Rolling Stone may receive an affiliate commission.

They say mother knows best, and when it comes to gift-giving, mine really does. My mom has always had a flair for whimsy. She’s the kind of woman who would lay an entire tarp across our backyard on the last day of school and stage a food fight, or build a Christmas countdown calendar from scratch, then fill every little compartment with surprises.

She’s also the reason I learned that the best gifts aren’t necessarily the ones people ask for, but the ones they didn’t even know they needed. Turns out, that lesson would come in handy when I made gift-giving my career.

My mom is truly the best human on the planet. Don’t worry, I’m sure yours is, too. Which is why I want to help you find a present she’ll love when the holidays roll around.

So, where do you start shopping? I’d steer clear of the obvious. Sure, a robe is nice. A scarf will keep her warm. Another water bottle? She probably has six. You can do better! This is the woman who taught you how to make the most out of life, after all. She deserves something as one-of-a-kind as your relationship. Maybe that’s a cuckoo clock you can build together or a custom garden gnome with her face on it. Whatever fills her cup, there’s something here that’ll make her smile.

I also kept budgets, interests, and personalities in mind, because no two moms — or gift-givers — are alike. Hanukkah, Christmas, and some of the biggest shopping holidays of the year are fast approaching (and didn’t your mom teach you that early is always better than on time?). So consider this your friendly nudge to get shopping. For Hanukkah, aim to have your gifts sorted by around Nov. 20, roughly two weeks before the holiday. For Christmas, I’d try to wrap things up by Dec. 12.

Shop my 35+ best gifts for your mom below, and give her something she’ll actually want to show off to her friends. You know, something other than another photo of you.

Cozy Earth pajamas have been one of the most popular products in all of our holiday gift guides, and for good reason. Between their near-unreasonable level of softness and my own personal devotion to them, they had to make this list too. They run true to size, but if you’re debating sizing up, I say go for it. The worst thing that can happen is she ends up even more comfortable.

Gorjana makes stunning birthstone bracelets that you can customize with stones representing her kids, grandkids, or whoever else has earned a spot. Or, you can leave everyone else off and just include yourself… A subtle way to remind her who her favorite child is.

If your mom spends as much time in the garden as she does with your dad — potentially more, no judgment — get her a custom garden gnome with her face on it. All you have to do is provide a photo, and Printerval handles the rest.

Have you hugged your mom recently? If not, I’m going to need you to get on that. But even the most devoted children can’t be there all the time, and sometimes she might need a little extra comfort when you’re not around. This Bearaby weighted blanket is a pretty good stand-in. Just don’t let it replace an actual phone call.

This might be one of the sweetest gifts you can buy your mom this holiday season because chances are she owned a version of this Lenox Spice Village when she was little, or her mom did. Either way, it’s the kind of nostalgic gift that can transport her back to simpler times while also giving her kitchen a very cute upgrade. My mother-in-law had the original, so I got her this modern version for Christmas this year (and Cara, if you see this, pretend you didn’t).

If you thought Tommy Bahama only made resort wear for vacations in the Bahamas, allow me to introduce you to the brand’s winter staples. This fleece jacket is insanely comfortable, and I own multiple Tommy Bahama sweaters that have convinced me the quality is the real deal. Take my word for it; I shop for a living.

My mom reads about three books a week, no exaggeration, so making sure she never loses her place is practically a public service. I got her this page-saving cottage lamp, which doubles as a cute little bedside accessory. It adds a bit of whimsy to her literary world and means she can stop folding the corners of her books. (Please tell her not to do that.)

And since she’s so obsessed with reading, I also got her this literature mug from Uncommon Goods, which happens to be one of my favorite places to find genuinely unique gifts. She just has to scan it with her phone (and if she doesn’t have an upgraded smartphone yet, maybe that’s your next gift) to hear from some of the world’s greatest literary minds whenever she wants.

Treat your mom to tea for two (with you, obviously) by buying her this stunning Spode china set. It’s currently on sale at Macy’s, and as someone who was recently gifted her own set of nice china, I can tell you that opening a box like this might just take her breath away. Which, really, is how you win Christmas. Not that it’s a competition…

For that beautiful china you just got her, help her preserve it with this stunning display cabinet from Ashley Furniture. It fits perfectly into just about any corner of her home and gives her a place to show off all of her favorite mementos. Maybe your eighth-grade art project won’t make the cut, but her mother’s wedding spoon might.

So, yes, this might be one of the more basic Christmas gifts on the list, but you seriously can’t go wrong with getting your mom a pair of house shoes. And if you’re going to do it, make them UGGs. They’re a chic way to give her slippers that feel a little more thoughtful than the random pair you panic-bought from a massive online retailer two days before Christmas.

This is a more practical gift for your mom, but it’s also one she probably didn’t know existed or that she needed. Memory Air works by releasing scented puffs while she sleeps and claims to do everything from helping her get more ZZZs at night to supporting memory, according to the brand. Plus, it’s backed by Doctors, so your mom doesn’t have to worry that this is another random thing you were served in a suspicious Instagram ad. I regret the minute I ever told my mom about those.

For the moms who love birdwatching, this high-tech feeder lets her see who’s making a pit stop through a connected app. It can even help identify the species, which is a whole lot easier than trying to describe a bird to an AI website and hoping for the right answer.

My mom has been making us pajama pants for Christmas for as long as I can remember, so I recently decided to upgrade her sewing machine and make her job a little easier. She hasn’t stopped telling me how much she loves this model. It’s great for easy projects, more challenging ones, and even the things she didn’t know were possible. Her mom used a Singer, my mom uses a Singer, and I use a Singer, too. Three generations in, I think we can call that a family endorsement.

My mom and I went to Austria together in September and walked into a cuckoo clock shop. We both knew immediately that we had to buy one. If your mom shares the same wandering spirit as the two of us, get her one she can build herself — or, even better, one you can help her put together. Consider it an excuse to spend a little more time together.

If your mom is the kind of woman who would happily spend the rest of her life in comfortable clothes, you’re doing her a disservice by not getting her this Aviator Nation sweatshirt and matching sweatpants. It delivers everything she loves about her favorite sweatshirt, just in a version she can actually leave the house in. The one with your childhood fingerprints all over it is cute, but maybe it’s time to move that one into the memory box.

Tinggly is one of my personal gifting hacks because it gives your mom more than another thing to unwrap under the tree; it gives her an experience. And, realistically, the thing she probably wants most is to spend time with you. A weekend getaway is a pretty great way to make that happen. This package lets her choose from multiple destinations, so you can give her the R&R she deserves without pretending you know exactly where she wants to go.

If I had to guess, your mom has been carrying the same tired shoulder bag for approximately a decade. Give her a much-deserved upgrade with this Rebecca Minkoff and Grace Ann Nader hobo bag. Bonus points if she’s a Real Housewives fan, since Rebecca Minkoff is a RHONY alum.

My mom is always asking me about my skincare routine, and instead of giving her my full Get Ready With Me speech every time she brings it up, I finally just sent her the link to this Water Whip moisturizer. It’s a no-frills but extremely functional way to keep your skin looking close to perfect, and now she uses it every morning and, apparently, has made it her personal mission to tell all of her friends about it too.

Even dog moms are mothers, so if you’re shopping for the woman in your life who treats her pet like her actual baby (as she should), this Lola dog bed (yes, that Lola) will help her spoil her little furry bundle of joy accordingly. Since getting one for my dog, he hasn’t left it for more than 20 minutes at a time. Plus, I appreciate how good it actually looks in my home, which is something I can’t say about every dog bed.

Brilliant Earth has some of our favorite gift ideas for Mom, but this special necklace is as tasteful as it gets. It’s dainty, affordable, and best of all, it comes from you. Pearls supposedly symbolize motherhood and everlasting love, so what better way to put that sentiment into something tangible than with this pendant?

I recently got my mom this bedding bundle, and one morning I woke up to a text that read: “OMG, OMG, OMG, this is the best set of sheets ever!!!! I didn’t want to get out of bed!! Do I have to go to work?? LOL.” True story. So, if you want to wake up to a similarly enthusiastic review from your own mother, this is a pretty safe bet.

Myles Price makes sweatshirts for every kind of mom, especially the hot mamas who want to be comfortable but still look like they have their lives together. She’ll be the envy of every other parent at school pickup, and almost definitely the most comfortable one there.

I feel a little guilty about this one. I’ve known about Lola Blankets for a while now, own multiple, and am about as obsessed with them as the rest of the internet. Then my mom called me the other day to say she saw one on TV and was dying to get one…. Something I probably should have gotten her months ago. Well, I immediately ordered it, and now it’s on its way. Learn from my mistakes and do the same for your mother before it’s too late and they sell out of her Christmas list.

My mom has been wearing this scent for decades, and she always told me that you can never go wrong with Chanel. You know what? She was right (as she is about a lot of things). This was also reportedly one of Marilyn Monroe’s favorite scents, and while some gentlemen might prefer blondes, your mom will probably prefer this perfume.

Getting your mom another picture of yourself is so last year. What she really wants under the tree is a portrait of her dog as a member of the royal court. I was gifted one of these a few years ago and have been slowly getting them for all of my family members ever since. My mom hangs hers in what she calls her parlor, also known as the billiards room. Whatever you call the room, this deserves a spot in it.

For the moms who are fans of The Mary Tyler Moore Show, you can now get her some of the most iconic looks she saw on screen, thanks to this collaboration with Frances Valentine. How’s that for actually paying attention to her hobbies and getting her something she probably wouldn’t think to buy herself?

If you have a wife who recently became a mom, get her these Hot Mom socks so she can remember exactly who the hell she is, even in the thick of postpartum life. Did I send this link to my brother so he could get them for his wife, who just had a baby? Yes. Do I know she’ll appreciate the reminder? More than you think. Take it from a woman.

My mom and I ran our first-ever 5K in Brooks sneakers, and while we might not be adding marathons to our regularly scheduled mother-daughter activities anytime soon, one thing we’ll continue doing together is wearing Brooks. They’re the only sneakers my mom will buy, and considering she works retail and spends eight hours a day on her feet, I trust her judgment.

Your mom is already the head of your family’s team, but if she has another team she roots for — or a specific player she loves, like Josh Allen (all moms love him, it’s a fact) — help her show her support with a cute sweatshirt from ’47 and Stoney Clover Lane. They run slightly bigger than your average crewneck, so size down if she prefers a more fitted look.

Moms everywhere are obsessed with mahjong. That might be a generalization, but I know mine is, as are all of my aunts and cousins who are moms. Help her get in on the game-night action with a set of her very own. She’ll be the most popular person at the next family gathering.

I always say you should buy your mom a pair of recovery slides from Nike because they help her take a load off, quite literally. But then I found this upgraded version from Nike x Hyperice that actually heats up and massages her feet. Yeah, that’s an automatic win.

If your mom is obsessed with all things Disney, pay tribute to one of her favorite pastimes with this Lilly Pulitzer and Disney dress. It’s a little less Mickey Mouse and a little more subtly infused magic, thanks to a classic Lilly Pulitzer silhouette she can wear outside the parks, too.

My mom is always cold. She probably doesn’t realize that she spent 20 years of her life sitting through my brothers’ ice hockey games, but I’m pretty sure that’s where it started. So I got her this Carhartt jacket, which manages to keep her at just the right temperature even during a brutal New Hampshire winter. If anyone knows how to dress for a chilly day, it’s a hockey mom.

At some point in time, your mom will become obsessed with her family tree (if it hasn’t happened already). The day my mom told me we were distantly related to a European king was the greatest day of her life, which only mildly concerned me. Help her dig into her past with an Ancestry DNA kit. You never know who your mom might discover she’s related to, or which royal family she might try to claim as her own.

For the moms whose love language is a Sunday supper (which was definitely my nanny’s), get her these world-class pans from Eater and Heritage Steel. She can whip up anything, from meatballs to her own top-secret gravy recipe, and you’ll benefit from the results. Or, better yet, make her a meal with them. She deserves it, after all.

This gift is for both the moms who love to garden and those who want to but might not exactly have a green thumb. I’ll tell you this: When I first bought one for myself, I was skeptical. But it supplied my husband and me with six months of the freshest vegetables I’ve ever tasted, after which I immediately ordered more seedling pods. When my parents found out about it, they ordered one, too, and my mom says she’d never go back to traditional gardening. That’s a pretty strong endorsement.

Previously, Mikulski Ruiz contributed to Men’s Health, Good Housekeeping, Oprah Daily, Women’s Health, and more. GdS: A midfielder with a handbrake – Sylla’s journey to USG and why Milan are interested

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

AC Milan have been considering a move for Massire Sylla recently and the Senegalese defender’s stock is only rising currently.

There are plenty of defensive options for Milan in the winter mercato. Goncalo Inacio is probably the preference for most, but the profile of Sylla cannot be ignored. Another signing for the future, he’s similar in many ways to Sankhoun Diawara, and the Frenchman has already started to have an impact.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

Gazzetta dello Sport have looked a little at his background and his profile. A physical defender, he has previously admitted that he’s a lover of ‘the fight’; a player who loves duels and wins them a lot of the time.

Called up to the Senegalese national team this month, his story is one of patience, tenacity and the ability to persist when faced with obstacles. His career started at Diambars Academy, one of the most notorious in Senegal, starting as a midfielder but eventually moving back to defence.

His coaches wanted him to learn to play with his feet, but his defensive qualities shone, hence the move back. With that, though, he started to see the ball more and both aspects of his game improved substantially.

From there, he joined Pau in 2024 and within six months, he was playing for the senior team in Ligue 2. At 19, he moved to Lyn and then Union Saint-Gilloise paid €2 million for him last January.

It wasn’t an immediate fit, though, and he was called overexcited, thanks to his yellow card record. Yet, he’s settled and now is a key figure in a three-man defence and picking out his best traits are difficult because there are so many.

‘It tends to tick people off’: How the U.S.’s biggest news union is fighting an AI onslaught

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A decade ago, while working as a data reporter at the Los Angeles Times, Jon Schleuss watched a new boss take control of the paper’s parent company, rename it Tronc, and promise to supercharge the business by repackaging the paper’s “content” across an array of media. Instead, leadership began “running it into the ground,” says Schleuss. “We decided to fight.” He soon helped organize the newsroom’s first union. Now, as president of the NewsGuild-CWA, the largest news union in the United States, Schleuss is hearing a new version of the same story, this time powered by AI.

Case in point is USA Today Co., formerly Gannett, the country’s largest newspaper chain. In August, the company announced a partnership with Palantir—the data analytics giant whose clients include Nvidia, U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE), and Britain’s National Health Service—to better monetize its audience and content. It’s part of a broader effort that includes push-button videos, AI-written article summaries, and even whole articles. That sort of automation echoes moves across the media industry.

NewsGuild members at USA Today papers have demanded an end to the Palantir deal, citing reader trust and a conflict of interest for reporters who cover ICE. The union has also pushed back on some of the company’s artificial intelligence initiatives amid continuing layoffs. Last month, after the rollout of a new reader-facing chatbot across USA Today websites, guild journalists at two papers in Ohio pulled their bylines; among its hallucinations, they said, the bot misidentified the reporter covering a murder case as the suspect.

In an interview in September, Mike Reed, the USA Today CEO, told Fast Company that the company won’t replace journalists with AI. But unlike Ziff Davis, IGN, The New Republic, Time, and CalMatters, it hasn’t put that promise in a contract—something that NewsGuild journalists are now demanding in ongoing negotiations.

The layoffs keep coming, too, even in cases where contracts include AI protections. (The Local Journalist Index estimates that the U.S. has lost about 80% of its local journalists since the turn of the century, from about 40 per 100,000 residents in 2002 to just 7.8 today.)

But the upheaval is also fueling an organizing surge, one Schleuss says the union hasn’t seen since its founding in 1933 as the American Newspaper Guild, during the Great Depression. In the time that he has been president, the guild has added more than 10,000 workers from 246 workplaces—not just journalists but people in the publishing, tech, and nonprofit worlds. (Reporters at Fast Company and Inc. unionized with the Writers Guild of America East in 2018.) “You see CEOs that make millions of dollars and don’t increase your pay and want to lay you off and speak in this weird, AI-written code-speak,” Schleuss says. “It tends to tick people off when the price of groceries is just through the roof.”

Schleuss spoke with Fast Company about the industry’s embrace of AI, the growing labor movement in journalism, and what it will take to protect reporters—and the work they do. The conversation has been edited for length and clarity.

Generative AI is being rolled out all over the place, from healthcare to hiring to scammy content creating. Why is the discussion about AI in journalism so critical?

The news industry is the only industry mentioned in the Constitution—we can start there. A free press is essential to having a democracy, and so if you replace the workers who provide that free press with bots, you get misinformation. 

Our currency is trust. And you can’t do that if you’ve got corporations that are just spewing out word calculators to replace those working journalists who have ethical responsibilities: seeking truth and reporting it, minimizing harm, acting accountable and independent, and being transparent. Those are all our key ethical concerns, and if we stray from them too far, then we lose faith, we lose trust, we lose our free press, we lose our democracy. So I’d say it’s all a pretty straight through line: Without journalists, we don’t have democracies, and replacing journalists with AI is a very quick way to get rid of the democracy. Which might be a goal.

Let’s start with USA Today Co., formerly Gannett, the biggest newspaper chain. How are journalists there seeing AI get rolled out in its newsrooms?

They’re pushing AI on people. One tool, Espresso, will take a press release and then try to spit out a story. But I don’t know, it seems really dangerous if the Nazis are emailing you a press release and you just make a quote-unquote story about it. They’re also trying to do that instead of hiring humans to write a story. So it becomes this one-sided slop that journalists are then forced to edit in as quick an amount of time as possible.

That’s not exactly journalism.

You have to approach AI with journalistic ethics, which I think is Gannett’s real problem. And that’s been the challenge all along: How do you make sure that we are doing the things necessary as journalists to be ethical when we’re using these systems? And that requires seeking the actual truth and reporting it, and not just relying on the output from a single-sourced community news announcement that was emailed to you. It requires actually fact-checking things. That requires going through any data that’s being analyzed to verify that these things that you’re saying are true.

Fact-checking is already a heavy lift. And more AI often means more checking. With fewer journalists and more AI, don’t we risk automating away the very things that we need to make the AI work? To make facts and society work. It’s like, remember why we are doing this to begin with. Why we are reading things.

Do you remember when the company Tronc was created? That was my life. I lived and worked for the L.A. Times, which was owned by Tronc. And its chairman at the time, Michael Ferro, was like, “We’re just going to put the content in different funnels.” It’s basically, I think, what [USA Today CEO] Mike Reed’s strategy is today. And it was a failed strategy. It’s like he doesn’t think about the work that journalists do as telling stories. He thinks of it as content. This is the thing that is just so broken to so many media executives. They think of content; they don’t think of stories. It’s absolutely tone deaf and 10 years old.

The thing that infuriates me: You go to any of the USA Today websites now, and the websites are broken. I wish that he would spend more time working on actual user interfaces. Try not to have so many ads that are autoplaying, because if you’re a reader and you’re trying to read the news, you are holding onto a bucking bronco trying to follow the words on the screen. Everything’s moving around so fast. 

To me, the crux of the issue is: This is all about trust with readers. And if you align yourself with a very shady and dark company that has actively supported an agency that has hurt journalists, your readers are not going to trust you as much. And the latest polling shows that 52% of Americans are more concerned about AI than excited about it. It just seems such a bad move, when trust in journalism’s already at such a record low, for a journalism organization to then lean into AI. 

What else do you think USA Today’s leaders are getting wrong? And how is the union responding?

There’s the part of his interview with you where Mike Reed says AI won’t replace journalists: “I think the Guild plants this, as well as outside cynics, that we’re going to replace journalists with AI. That couldn’t be further from the truth.” But the reality is, we have 95 contracts [across the union] that have AI language in them, and the best-case-scenario ones say the employer will not lay off workers because of AI. But Gannett has refused to agree to any of that language. 

What’s the status of those conversations now? 

We’ve got 46 different contracts at Gannett, so it depends on where each of them are. They’re still bargaining for a first contract at The Columbus Dispatch—they just unionized this year. But everyone’s been fighting for no layoffs because of AI. Mike Reed’s refused to agree to that, but then they’ve also actually laid people off. 

What’s some of the most interesting AI-related language you’ve seen in other newsroom contracts?

What’s one of the funnest ones that’s super simple? The New Republic in December of ’24, says: “Generative AI may be used by bargaining unit employees as a complementary tool in editorial work, but it may not be used as a primary tool for creation of such. The use of AI shall not result in the layoff of a bargaining unit employee, be used to avoid filling any vacant bargaining unit position, or result in an employee’s compensation decreasing.” 

There was also a lefty magazine, The American Independent, and its contract said, simply: “Employees shall not use AI to produce any published material, with the exception of quotes derived from interview transcripts . . . or shall not utilize or approve the use of AI to produce editorial content for publication.” It was aggressive. 

What is the guild aiming for generally in its contracts?

The five major points that we fight for in contracts are: 

Number one is no layoffs because of AI. 

Number two is no salary reductions because of AI—another thing that Gannett has refused to agree to. 

Number three is usually the formation of a labor-management committee so that issues can be worked out in real time. This has been, frankly, really successful and could be successful at Gannett, but journalists there have kept away from it.

Number four is just making sure that you don’t do any impersonation of journalists. Don’t train off of their work or impersonate them without their permission. 

And the fifth and final one is really important for our readers: If you’re going to use AI to generate something, you need to label it.

When we spoke, Reed made it sound like that was a policy, that they would be clear if they were using AI.

The policy is adorable, but it’s not legally binding. It’s the AI language you actually want in a collective bargaining agreement—because if you don’t have a union, then you get no say. 

What they typically push for from the management side is saying something like, “AI may be used to generate news content that is supplementary to local news reporting and is not a replacement for it.” And then they’ll say, “The use of AI by all parties shall be consistent with the ethical guidelines and policies for Gannett journalists regarding AI-generated or assisted content.” But there’s nothing, at least in these contracts that I’m looking at, that says anything about labeling. So it might be in that guideline that they put out, but again, if they can just change that, they will. 

There’s a really good bill that’s sitting on [Governor] Kathy Hochul’s desk in New York called the New York FAIR News Act. It would require labeling for anything that was wholly generated using AI on a news website. Publishers are freaking out about it. They’re like, “Well, this is a violation of the 1st Amendment.” In 1933, whenever you didn’t want journalists to organize a union, you claimed the 1st Amendment protected you. But you have to put terms of service on [your website]. You have to have a privacy policy on your website. Labeling AI content on your news website shouldn’t be bad. It seems basic. 

What happens when AI gets better at simulating journalism, and the disclaimers disappear?

It’s already gotten better at writing, but the problem is, I think, people have also gotten better at seeing it. And even if it’s like, “Is this Coke or is this Coke Zero?”—people then are just assuming it’s all shit. So that’s also a problem. Actually, it would be so much more productive to say: We are not in support of AI. That would actually resonate with people, because it’d be news without AI. I think that you could actually wholly market a news organization off of that.

I hate this, because I’m a big believer in the open internet, but I actually think that news organizations right now need to create siloed universes where people will build reputational trust with an organization, because it’s not AI slop. Within five years, the majority of things we see on the internet will just be AI slop. It’s already there. So why not create a website that people can actually trust, written and curated by humans?

And Reed was gesturing at that. At the idea of being this oasis of trust in a sea of slop.

Then why put out AI slop?

Let’s talk about their deal with Palantir. USA Today’s execs told me Palantir works only with “highly controlled” data and has no access to newsroom systems or reporters’ notes, and accused the union of spreading fear and falsehoods about the deal. What did you make of that? 

My general reaction was just one of disappointment that both [president of USA Today Media] Kristin Roberts and Mike Reed have been so far removed from working in a newsroom that they forgot what it was like to have an organization that’s powered by journalists. They came off as extremely defensive of the relationship with Palantir, which has actively been working for ICE and DHS [Department of Homeland Security], which is not only a major news topic right now, but also is an organization that has actively assaulted journalists in city streets in the United States. We’ve had to file three separate lawsuits on it because it’s been so bad. So it’s really disappointing that they can’t see how this is an ethical issue, that this is a safety issue, that there’s a security issue here. 

Palantir has worked with or is working with a number of media organizations, including Axel Springer and Fox News. What specifically are union members concerned about?

What are you actually doing with readers’ data here? What are you losing control over? And then they came out swinging in defense of what Palantir has access to in terms of their journalists’ data. They’ve only now offered some clarity in your interview, when they have refused to do it until this point. But there’s much that’s still unclear. 

I also wonder about how one goal of the partnership—targeting readers using “anonymized” data—impacts the relationship with readers. Of course, many media organizations target their audiences with ads.

Reporters in the group chat say they’ve heard from readers who are really concerned about this relationship, what it means about their data. So that’s been an open question. 

Instead of using Palantir, they could find a good data journalist who could look at Google Analytics and help you figure out that this actually does better here and that does better there. And this webpage is taking three minutes to load: Maybe we should fix that?

I think some of the best things that work for us—this is so basic that it sounds stupid—is literally creating decently written newsletters that bring readers in and center their concerns. Here’s this newsletter from a local reporter; here’s a human being who wrote this. And it’s not just headlines and links. It’s literally, this is what happened in your neighborhood in New York City over the last day. That’s what people are hungry for, and it’s not rocket science. You just create and curate communities where people actually want to congregate and learn.

What else have journalists been pushing for specifically when it comes to AI in the newsroom?

So there’s notification—this is what we won the Politico arbitration over. Politico didn’t notify its workers and journalists that they were rolling out new tools. One was a report builder tool, and it was supposed to be based off of the archive. And our members asked it, “Is abortion legal in the United States of America?” And it was like, “Yes, ever since Roe v. Wade.” And it just totally missed the story, broken by its own journalists, that showed that the Supreme Court overturned Roe v. Wade. So we filed arbitration for that one, and they shut down the tool—they had violated the contract, which required 60 calendar days’ notice of any new tool.

And how about compensating journalists if their work has been used in AI training and inference? 

The compensation piece has been a real challenge. There’s not been a company, a news company, that’s agreed to compensate journalists. There’s a prohibition on training off of work by a journalist if they don’t get their consent. So you need consent if you’re going to have anything trained. 

The New York Times [Company], for instance, has a deal with Amazon for AI and their content, and they have refused to provide compensation to New York Times journalists. Which is wild, because if a newspaper in Brazil picks up a story from the Times, the journalists get compensated for it.

It seems like the media companies are still trying to figure out the compensation schemes themselves. Isn’t some kind of compensation essential to the future of the business?

So many of these news companies are entering these dumb deals with AI companies, though they don’t know how much the Atlantic deal is worth versus the New York Times deal versus the McClatchy or the Gannett deal. They don’t know the cost. You’d think that they’d know how to bargain better, but they are bargaining in the dark and then accepting peanuts for something that these AI companies are just hungry for, which is original content. And that’s being stolen from all these journalists.

How do you think AI tools could be valuable for journalism?

I think that there could be some that were valuable if they were for a specific purpose. You look at The New York Times’s Manosphere Report, which basically looks at and transcribes hundreds of hours of podcasts from right-wing podcasters and then turns that into an internal email report that goes to Times journalists. That’s really helpful, because it saves you the time of having to listen to a lot of podcasts. And then it gives you a sense of, well, is there a particular trend that is happening in this particular area? Analyzing millions of records from Jeffrey Epstein being released by the government—that’s helpful. Transcribing interviews—that’s helpful. 

But it all needs to be checked. Every single one of these things has to be checked. You can’t just publish it and just say it’s the truth because it came from the machine. 

But back to Gannett. There’s another AI tool called Streamer, which essentially pulls from the archive and puts together short-form videos with AI-generated text and voiceover. The main problem, journalists say, is that it sucks. It’s not good. And there’s a 10-year-old internal tool that’s better than the AI tool.

Have you personally found a way to use AI that’s helpful, that’s reasonable?

In my union work, I have been unimpressed with its lack of accuracy. I could see ways, maybe, at some point, where it could be helpful to try and do comparisons of contracts, but I’ve just seen it fail.

Back when I was working at the L.A. Times, one of my coworkers was building a machine learning system to build a big database of recipes, because recipes are structured data. Then you can point the machine learning algorithm at the newspaper from a hundred years ago, at a PDF, and say: Figure out where the list of ingredients is, the ordered list of operations. That was a really helpful tool. And that was more than 10 years ago. 

I think organizing large sets of data is really interesting. Like the Epstein stuff. Or if you’ve got a large cache of, say, the archive of the Guild newspaper, and you want to find letters about constitutional questions. We just published our archive on our website, this printed newspaper that goes back to 1933. It’s 1,300 PDFs—huge. That’s an area where, if you’ve got a ton of documents, it’s actually helpful. Not to provide verified truths from the AI model, but to be able to say, “Well, how many times was automation mentioned in the 1930s?” And then have it directly cite, say, page 4 of this issue. And then you’d have to go back as a human and check it.

Kristin Roberts mentioned an internal chatbot at USA Today that helps reporters with FOIA [Freedom of Information Act] requests. Are there other ways that AI might be useful in newsrooms, or things that you’d like to see management do with it?

In my old job, I would scrape websites, like transit agencies and police departments. And I’d be creating a spreadsheet, so I’d have to write these scripts in Python. One thing that’s nice is you could actually speed that up a bit using AI. What would take me weeks and weeks to work on scraping is something that I could do much faster, because I’m able to use a system that has been trained on a ton of Stack Overflow pages and can say, here’s the programming language. It’s still important to know what the language that’s coming out says, so that you know it’s not just hacking the website or making things up. You’ve got to double-check it. 

And there’s definitely an argument, too, that we should just have more staff to be able to do it, because Mike Reed’s ultimate argument here is, this is going to free us up to do more things. But you’ve already cut the staff so much. You need to instead just hire staff to do the actual journalism, not try to find tools to fix a problem that you’ve created by cutting the staff so much. 

Beyond jobs, what are you hearing about AI’s impact on the process of reporting and writing itself?

Besides the jobs, I think an overarching issue is that a lot of the executives at news companies don’t actually understand what AI does, and they’re just trying to push it out. So we see that, whether it’s Axel Springer or McClatchy or Gannett, they’re all just these guys at the top who think that this is a good idea because they were with one of their millionaire friends who said this is a good thing, and they just believed them, and they don’t actually know what the systems are doing. 

I think the other thing in this moment of AI is, it actually makes it harder for journalists, because there’s so much AI slop and misinformation. And so it’s making it harder for journalists to know what’s true. 

Are there certain areas of coverage that newsrooms need to be paying more attention to now?

I think that having accountability reporting is really, really key right now, because when we lose journalists at the local level, that costs us money. When we don’t have someone who’s watching city council, watching our school boards, that means that the elected officials then can just do whatever the hell they want. So accountability is the big thing, and they’ve been shifting away from that. McClatchy has also done a major shift away from that, which has been really horrible. They laid off 30% of our represented membership in September. They got rid of a lot of accountability.

So how about, don’t lean into the very unpopular thing that people say is bad for society, and instead focus on humans that are telling stories and holding power to account. Because that’s been a working formula for thousands of years. Ever since we started grunting around as Neanderthals, we were telling stories.

InfoQ Homepage News Netflix Turns Operational Data Into a Knowledge Graph for AI-Driven Observability

Nov 13, 2026 3 min read

Netflix is developing an ontology-driven knowledge graph to accelerate production investigations and provide AI agents with a shared understanding of its operational environment. The platform connects information about applications, incidents, deployments, alerts, and infrastructure dependencies, addressing a problem that can leave engineers spending hours piecing together information scattered across different systems.

The company claims it handles more than 2 billion requests daily and upwards of 38 million real-time logging events every second. During the November 2024 Jake Paul-Mike Tyson fight, its infrastructure supported approximately 65 million concurrent streams. Yet investigating a typical application regression can take around four hours, with much of that time spent reconstructing relationships between systems rather than analysing the underlying data.

Netflix's solution is to make those relationships explicit through an operational ontology and knowledge graph, creating a foundation for automated root-cause analysis and, eventually, self-healing infrastructure.

Netflix already collects metrics, events, logs, and traces (MELT) across its applications and infrastructure. However, these signals are spread across systems such as alerting, incident management, deployment tooling, and engineering communication platforms.

An application regression might originate from a client release, an experiment, infrastructure load shedding, or a service several dependencies downstream. Although each system contains useful information, engineers must often connect the evidence manually.

Netflix identified the missing capability as a consistent way to describe not just individual components, but how they relate to one another.

Its Observability Ontology provides that common model, defining operational entities, their properties, and the relationships between them. It uses established standards including RDF, OWL, SHACL, and SPARQL, while the resulting knowledge graph is stored in QuipuDB, Netflix's distributed RDF database built on Rust's redb storage engine.

The graph is populated through a pipeline of components Netflix calls enrichers. These consume information from operational systems and transform it into structured relationships, represented as subject-predicate-object triples.

Some enrichment is deterministic, while other stages use LLMs and probabilistic techniques to interpret less structured information. Netflix constrains these outputs through strict validation rules, including consistent entity types, URI naming conventions, and checks against its software catalogue.

Netflix also captures information from incident discussions, including hypotheses, rejected explanations, and mitigation decisions. This allows human reasoning to become part of the operational knowledge base rather than disappearing when an incident conversation ends.

Its correlation engine examines 13 types of signals, including log errors, metric anomalies, traces, alerts, incidents, deployments, configuration changes, experiments, and code changes. It searches for potential causal relationships, evaluating evidence such as temporal proximity and severity to produce ranked hypotheses.

Netflix describes the process as a knowledge flywheel: Observe, Enrich, Infer, and Adapt. As investigations produce new insights, those relationships can inform future detection and analysis. The knowledge graph also underpins AutoRCA, Netflix's internal agentic AI system for root-cause analysis.

Rather than having specialist agents exchange loosely structured findings, they use the ontology as a common interface. Agents analysing traces, logs or deployments can contribute information about the same operational entities without needing to understand each other's internal implementations.

Netflix intends to use this foundation to produce ranked, evidence-backed explanations for incidents, with humans retaining approval authority over proposed changes and remediation.

Platforms such as Datadog, Dynatrace, and Splunk already correlate observability signals and increasingly incorporate AI into incident investigation. Netflix's approach emphasizes the semantic model behind those signals, making operational relationships persistent and directly accessible to both engineers and machines.

The company ultimately wants to extend this capability from automated root-cause analysis into automated remediation and self-healing infrastructure. It also plans to explore integration with its Unified Data Architecture, connecting operational information to broader business and technology domains

Canadian Solar Schedules Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call for December 3

KITCHENER, ON, Nov. 13, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Canadian Solar Inc. (the "Company" or "Canadian Solar") (NASDAQ: CSIQ) today announced that it will hold a conference call on Thursday, December 3, 2026, at 8:00 a.m. U.S. Eastern Time to discuss the Company's third quarter 2026 results and business outlook.

The dial-in phone number for the live audio call is +1-877-704-4453 (toll-free from the U.S. and Canada) or +1-201-389-0920 from international locations. The conference ID is 13763867. A live webcast of the conference call will also be available via the webcast link on the investor relations section of Canadian Solar's website.

A telephone replay of the call will be available after the conclusion of the call until 11:59 p.m. U.S. Eastern Time on Thursday, December 17, 2026, and can be accessed by dialing +1-844-512-2921 (toll-free from the U.S.) or +1-412-317-6671 from international locations. The replay access ID is 13763867. A webcast replay will also be available via the webcast link on the investor relations section of Canadian Solar's website.

Canadian Solar is one of the world's largest solar technology and renewable energy companies. Founded in 2001 and headquartered in Kitchener, Ontario, the Company is a leading manufacturer of solar photovoltaic modules; provider of solar energy and battery energy storage solutions; and developer, owner, and operator of utility-scale solar power and battery energy storage projects. Over the past 25 years, Canadian Solar has successfully delivered nearly 180 GW of premium-quality solar photovoltaic modules to customers across the world. Through its energy storage solutions business e-STORAGE, Canadian Solar has shipped over 23 GWh of battery energy storage solutions to global markets and had a contracted backlog of $3.5 billion as of June 30, 2026. Since entering the project development business in 2010, Canadian Solar has developed, built, and connected approximately 12.4 GWp of solar power projects and 6.4 GWh of battery energy storage projects globally. Its geographically diversified project development pipeline includes approximately 22 GWp of solar and 84 GWh of battery energy storage capacity in various stages of development. Canadian Solar is one of the most bankable companies in the solar and renewable energy industry, having been publicly listed on the NASDAQ since 2006. For additional information about the Company, follow Canadian Solar on LinkedIn or visit www.canadiansolar.com.

Duquesne’s Michael Buckley says young investors skip over learning these critical skills. They could prove to be invaluable in the AI boom

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As investors race to identify the next big winners in the AI boom, standing out requires more than just spotting the next breakthrough. Michael Buckley, managing director and head of equities at investment firm Duquesne Family Office, says young investors often overlook the fundamentals of corporate finance. In the age of AI, that could give them an edge.

Buckley has built a diverse investing résumé. He’s worked across private equity, venture capital, and public equities—and his early days in debt and credit helped set him apart. Right after graduating from Harvard, Buckley learned the ropes of accounting and corporate finance at Sankaty Advisors, the now-renamed credit arm of private equity giant Bain Capital. With credit as the focus, Buckley poured himself into capital and corporate structures, as well as bonds and loans. His responsibilities unwittingly set him up to be recruited for one of America’s top private investment firms.

“I learned the importance of things like capital structure and debt, which I think a lot of people—for example, that start out their career in things like equities—oftentimes will skip that stuff over,” Buckley tells Fortune. “It’s because it’s tedious and it can be complicated,” he added.

Early-career analysts who have their heads buried in stock charts, earnings growth, and revenue projections could get a leg-up by returning to those basics. There’s a simple reason: The AI boom hinges on debt. Buckley explains that in this new ecosystem, debt is suddenly very important not only to distressed debt companies, but also to public markets and venture firms. As AI companies and their infrastructure partners take on more borrowing to finance data centers, chips, and computing capacity, investors need to assess how quickly a business might grow—and whether they can afford to pay back what they owe. It particularly stands out for those involved in data centers or funding next-generation labs, Buckley adds; tech hyperscalers issued more than $100 billion in corporate bonds last year, according to the Bank for International Settlements.

Buckley recognizes that doors opened thanks to his debt and credit expertise. During his two-year stint as an analyst at private equity firm CPMG, a headhunter came calling; Duquesne was looking to hire a credit and equities wiz to work under the family office’s founder and leader, legendary macro investor Stanley Druckenmiller, whose claim to fame was helping George Soros make $1 billion betting against the British pound in 1992. He joined as an analyst at 27.

Today, at Duquesne, he’s steering some of the firm’s biggest wins. Over the past 11 years he’s leaned into investments in AI, as well as healthcare and biotech—which he believes will vastly innovate over the next decade. Recently, Buckley says the firm backed pharma company Formation Bio. As a self-proclaimed “non-dogmatic” investor, he’s continuing to keep his horizons open, a philosophy shared by Druckenmiller: Keeping a broad perspective on the market—and a set of skills to meet the moment—could mean the difference between spotting an opportunity or missing it entirely.

“Now, we’re seeing that in the AI venture world, these concerns about credit and interest rates suddenly have this big impact on AI infrastructure, private companies, and the venture community,” Buckley says. “I think it’s something I’ve learned from Stan, to always be thinking about the top-down and the bottom-up.”

Emma Burleigh is a reporter at Fortune, covering success, careers, entrepreneurship, and personal finance. Before joining the Success desk, she co-authored Fortune’s CHRO Daily newsletter, extensively covering the workplace and the future of jobs. Emma has also written for publications including the Observer and The China Project, publishing long-form stories on culture, entertainment, and geopolitics. She has a joint-master’s degree from New York University in Global Journalism and East Asian Studies.

The CEO's Real Job During An AI Transition

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

​Most conversations about AI leadership tend to sound alike, with transformation road maps and bold predictions about what the organization will look like in five years. That language sounds impressive, but it skips past the part of the job that keeps me up at night: how we get there without breaking what already works.

The tension few leaders admit is that you have to build the organization you’ll need 18 to 24 months from now while still running the one you have today. You cannot pause operations to retool. Customers still need service and payroll still needs to run. As a result, you really need to do two jobs at once, and most days they pull against each other.

I think of it as renovating a building while people still live in it. You can’t tear out the plumbing and tell everyone to come back in a year. You’re rerouting water while the taps still need to work. That means running temporary lines so nobody loses service and telling residents which faucet to use this week and why.

In an AI transition, the residents are your employees and your customers. The work still has to get done today, even as you change how it gets done tomorrow. The CEO’s job is to sequence that change and protect what’s working while it’s being replaced, while keeping people informed enough that they trust the renovation instead of fear it. It gets harder once you factor in the people doing the work, because they don’t all move at the same pace.

What surprised me most, even after years of leading organizations through change, is how unevenly adoption spreads across a company. Give the same tool, training and deadline to five departments and you will get five different response curves. Some teams run with it immediately, while others need more time. A few will quietly work around it and hope nobody notices.

The instinct, especially under pressure to show results, is to treat the slow adopters as a compliance issue: Send another email, set a harder deadline, loop in HR. But when someone with real skill and a track record of success is dragging their feet on a new way of working, often it’s because something else is going on: unclear expectations or a legitimate gap in the capabilities of the tool itself. Maybe it’s an unspoken fear, usually about what their job becomes once the tool works.

So I try to get curious before I get directive and ask teams what they see as the main barrier. Sometimes the answer is that the person needs more support to build a skill they don’t have yet. Sometimes they have the skill and the motivation and I just haven’t given them a clear enough reason to change something that already works for them. Those are different problems, and they require different responses. I’ve learned that neither gets solved by a companywide memo. Here is what I recommend leaders do instead.

Some leaders avoid or ignore the hardest conversation, which is what happens to people’s roles during AI tool adoption and transition. I’d rather have that conversation directly than let people fill the silence with worse assumptions. In my own organization, I’ve told people plainly that most of our jobs will look different within a year. I don’t mean they’ll disappear. But the work will shift from producing to reviewing, from doing the task to deciding whether the machine got it right.

That can be an uncomfortable thing to say to a team. It’s also more respectful than pretending it isn’t true. People can handle hard news. What wears them down is watching the ground shift underneath them while leadership insists nothing has changed.

Another part of the job that rarely makes it into strategy decks is deciding, in advance, what requires a human sign-off. Once you start giving AI real authority over workflows, whether that’s scheduling, communications or decisions that touch a customer, you need clear thresholds for when it acts on its own and when a human should intervene to stop and ask.

It’s better to over-define those boundaries early and loosen them as trust builds than to discover deep problems. More than anything, AI transitions are leadership and process decisions that need to be made by someone who understands both the operational stakes and the people affected by them.

When I think about what my job looks like during this AI transition (or any other major transition), a lot of it is managing friction day after day, and moving fast while keeping things stable. Some teams will be ready before others, and that’s okay.

We also must accept that there is often a difference between what technology can do and what people are ready to trust it with. A tool can be capable on day one, but adoption happens one workflow, one team, and one good experience at a time. Nobody can hand you a playbook for this because every organization’s friction points are different. The job is finding those and working through them without letting the taps run dry.

Navigating this well has everything to do with showing up week to week and having the uncomfortable conversations early, not worrying about five years down the line. It means treating every person’s resistance as its own puzzle instead of a compliance problem. So I keep checking the taps. Most days they work, and when one doesn’t, I want to hear about it early, and I want whoever tells me to know I’m glad they did. ​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?
