Microsoft Says ‘No Evidence of Malicious Activity’ in Global Outage of Outlook and Exchange Email as It Begins to Restore Services

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Microsoft on Friday was still struggling to restore service to millions of Outlook and Exchange email users more than 18 hours after its servers crashed and became unavailable.

In an update on Microsoft’s Service Health Status page Friday morning, the company did not explain what triggered the catastrophic outage but said that it did not suspect a cyberattack or other foul play.

“At this time, we have no evidence of malicious activity associated with this incident,” the company said in the update at 6:11 a.m. ET on Nov. 13. “We will continue to treat this with urgency until full mitigation, and we will share a full Post-Incident Report with root cause and defense-in-depth actions after mitigation is complete.” Related Stories Michael John Roberts, Animator and Director on 'Bojack Horseman,' 'Triptank,' Dies at 48 The Best Disney Merch to Gift in 2026: Blu-ray Box Sets, Legos, Blind Boxes, Video Games and More

Outlook and other Microsoft 365 services that interact with the Exchange Online and email services remained largely unavailable, but as of Friday some users had begun to regain access to their inboxes. The issues began affecting Outlook at around 12:30 p.m. ET on Thursday; both individual users and enterprise customers have been unable to access their email since then.

On Friday, Microsoft said that “We’ve made additional progress toward service recovery, with recovery actions completed across portions of the affected infrastructure in the Asia Pacific region. Traffic is being gradually restored, and we’re seeing improvements in key customer scenarios, including mailbox access and delayed email processing. However, some users may continue to experience intermittent issues.”

The tech company continued, “Our focus is now on extending these recovery actions across affected infrastructure in other regions, while continuing stabilization efforts and adding supporting capacity to improve service consistency. Reducing email queues and backlogs remains a priority, and the extended 48-hour email timeout window remains in place to help protect delayed messages as delivery catches up.”

At 7 a.m. ET, Microsoft said in an update, “We’re seeing encouraging recovery progress in Europe, with service availability and email delivery performance improving across a growing portion of affected Microsoft 365 infrastructure. Japan remains comparatively more stable, while North America is also improving; however, some users may still experience intermittent issues as recovery actions continue.”

Applications and features of multiple Microsoft 365 services that interact with Exchange Online and email services were affected. Those included Outlook, the Microsoft 365 admin center, Microsoft 365 apps (as users may be unable to view their recently used files); Microsoft Copilot 365; SharePoint Online; and Microsoft Teams (with respect to features that depend on Exchange Online, including calendar-related failures with scheduling, invites, viewing and meeting edits and creation of Live or Virtual events).

“You Guys Are F***ing Idiots”: Jeff Kaplan Takes Aim At Blizzard’s Overwatch 2 Reveal

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Saying Overwatch 2 has had a rocky road since its 2019 reveal would be an understatement, and former Overwatch director Jeff Kaplan isn't a fan of how the game was first announced.

Speaking to Gaming World Interviews, Kaplan didn't mince words about the BlizzCon 2019 opening ceremony, where Overwatch 2 was shown for the first time, as Blizzard promised numerous features it either walked back or didn't deliver.

Kaplan said that from 2018 until his departure, he experienced constant friction with Blizzard's executive leadership over game direction, scheduling, and core game mechanics. This led him to confront leadership about the lack of a clear vision or business model for Overwatch 2, and to question the wisdom of announcing Overwatch 2 at the same time as Diablo 4.

"I got in a huge fight with, at the time, it wasn't Mike [Morhaime]. I loved Mike, who was the president of Blizzard, and another president of Blizzard, my executive producer, over announcing Overwatch 2. I went into their office, I’m like, 'What are we doing? We don’t really know what this game is; we don’t know what the business model is. This is a huge mistake.'"

According to Kaplan, the new executives "had a lot to prove" when they joined Blizzard. "They wanted to prove that 'Blizzard's back and Blizzard still has it.' And I'm like, 'You guys are f***ing idiots,' you know? I don’t know how else to put it, but after three years of just getting worn down and not agreeing with any of the decisions that were being made on the franchise and not feeling like I had agency anymore--like before it used to be like I'm the game director I get to decide the direction the game goes. And starting in 2018, I lost that, and after three years of fighting for it back, I realized this company belongs to these corporate people now, the Blizzard executive team, the Activision team."

Kaplan left Blizzard in 2021, saying Activision Blizzard’s financial arm told him that if Overwatch didn't meet certain financial goals, 1,000 people at the company would be laid off and the blame would fall on him. "The biggest f**k you moment I’ve had in my career--it felt surreal to be in that condition," Kaplan said earlier this year.

These days, Kaplan is working on The Legend of California at the new studio he co-founded, Kintsugiyama. The Blizzard veteran recently spoke to GameSpot about the "humbling" reality of being an indie dev and more.

Specialized’s Tarmac SL9 S-Level is built to wreck your Zone 2 ride

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Specialized’s second-fastest bike may be the smart buy.

I love a good Zone 2 ride. There’s nothing like getting on the bike, settling into a groove, and letting the miles (and hours) flow by. But sometimes when I hopped on the Specialized Tarmac SL9 S-Level, Zone 2 would turn into a tempo ride. It was like having a little imp—the stereotype dressed in red with the pitchfork—on my left shoulder whispering into my ear: “Go fast. This bike was built to go fast. Speed is good. You can do a Zone 2 ride tomorrow.” Normally, I’d count on the good imp—the dude with the harp and wings—on my right shoulder to restrain these urges. But the Tarmac is so seductive that he would just shrug and say “he’s got a point.” And in a moment of weakness, I would drop the hammer and head into the red, just because the SL9 makes that so rewarding.

The Tarmac SL9 is Specialized’s aero road bike, with the S-Level sitting just below the S-Works in Specialized’s top tier of carbon, aero road bikes, where it replaced the Tarmac SL8 Pro. After it got an update this year, I spent several weeks riding it, putting nearly 1,000 miles on it

Specialized offers the Tarmac SL9 in four models, all available with SRAM and Shimano drivetrains (Ars tested the SRAM version). Notably, you don’t need to buy the top-of-the-line bike to get the top-of-the-line groupset. Here’s the breakdown of the Tarmac SL9 lineup (note that the Shimano versions of the Expert is 20 g lighter and Comp is 70 grams heavier than the SRAM versions):

The $10,500 S-Level offers the top-of-the-line SRAM Red groupset and integrated cockpit, just like the $14,000 S-Works. But the frameset, wheelset, and saddle differ. The major difference is the frame: the S-Level gets the same carbon-fiber FACT 10r frame as the Expert and Comp. At 822 g for a 56 cm frame, the FACT 10r is 135 g heavier than the FACT 12r used on the S-Works. Despite the weight difference, Specialized says the FACT 10r offers the same stiffness and comfort characteristics as its lighter sibling. Along with the frame, different wheelsets and saddles make the S-Level 500 g heavier than the S-Works. With bottle cages, a Garmin mount, and my preferred Shimano XTR XC PD-M9200 MTB pedals, the S-Level I tested weighed in at 7.98 kg. By way of comparison, my own road bike—a Gen 8 Trek Madone—weighs in at 7.71 kg.

I rode the S-Level all around the Chicago suburbs as well as in central Illinois. Unlike the Diverge 4 Pro, I wasn’t able to test its climbing ability in the Rocky Mountains. All we’ve got in most of Illinois are short, punchy climbs … and a lot of long flats. I rode The S-Level on everything from freshly laid asphalt to freshly laid chipseal, including a couple of 60-mile-plus rides on the country roads downstate.

Visually, the S-Level is striking. The blue-on-white with gray striping is one of Specialized’s better-looking colorways. And it looks aerodynamic. Indeed, Specialized says the Tarmac SL9 is the most aerodynamic road bike it has ever made. Notable changes from the previous-generation Tarmac SL8 to SL9 involved tweaking the head tube, rerouting the rear hydraulic brake hose to accommodate the narrower head tube, and moving to a dropped-down tube for improved aerodynamics. But the most visually striking change is the Win Fin. As the seat tube rises from the bottom bracket, it deviates from the vertical to follow the curve of the wheel as it approaches the seat stays. When riding with just a single water bottle on the downtube—common in pro races where a full bottle is just a team car away—Specialized claims Win Fin saves riders half a watt.

The S-Level I tested came with Specialized’s 30 mm Specialized Cotton TLR tires—used by Red Bull-BORA-hansgrohe on the UCI World Tour—in a tubeless setup. According to Bicycle Rolling Resistance, the Cotton TLR outperforms the much-loved Continental GP 5000 S TR tires when it comes to rolling resistance (at 28 mm). Using Silca’s tire pressure calculator, I rode with the tires inflated to 73 psi in the front and 75 psi in the rear, a departure from the 62/64 setting on my 32 mm Contis—and there is a slight, but noticeable difference in ride comfort between the S-Level and my own road bike. Mounted on the Roval Rapide CL III carbon wheels with 21 mm internal width, the Cotton TLR tires measured 30.2 mm wide (my 32 mm Contis are a hair over 33 mm).

On every surface shy of brand-new chipseal, the Tarmac felt comfortable and balanced, with the bike absorbing some of the punishment meted out by uneven pavement. Fresh chipseal is another matter entirely, as it feels more like gravel, which felt jarring on the SL9 S-Level. What was particularly impressive about the SL9 is how great it feels on long rides. Some manufacturers are skilled at building aero bikes that feel great on hours-long rides, and the Tarmac is just as good as the Trek Madone SLR 9 AXS Gen 8 I reviewed in this regard.

When you’re ready to drop the hammer, the S-Level is your willing accomplice. It’s twitchy in all of the good ways, with instantaneous response to the rider’s effort. The SRAM Red AXS groupset makes shifting buttery smooth (and the bonus buttons on the brake hoods allow the rider to change screens on the bike computer without releasing the brake hoods), and the drivetrain (50t/37t chainrings and a 10t-36t cassette) offers gear ratios for every type of terrain.

The first few seconds of a climb on the Tarmac SL9 S-Level feel like they come for free. Whether I stayed seated or cranked out watts in an upright position, there was no lag or flex as the bike snapped forward. Descents were amazing, and cornering on the S-Level is sublime—I felt balanced and in control at all times.

There are a few nits to pick. The reach to the brakes in the drops felt excessive on my first couple of rides, and I had to adjust them slightly to brake easily on descents. Also, I would prefer shorter cranks on the Tarmac. The trend among professional riders has been toward shorter cranks because it makes holding an aero position easier and reduces hip and knee flexion. To its credit, Specialized reduced the crank length from 172.5 mm on the 56 cm SL8 to 170 mm on the same-sized SL9, but I run 165 mm cranks on my road bikes and noticed the difference on the S-Level. One demographic that buys expensive road bikes is middle-aged dudes with empty nests and disposable incomes, and they are the ones most likely to appreciate shorter cranks.

The 3D-printed Power Pro Mirror is a fine saddle, but there’s a reason I ride with a Specialized S-Works Power with Mirror on my Trek—it’s more comfortable and offers noticeably more padding around the cutout. And if I owned the S-Level, I’d move to 32 mm tires as soon as I wore out the 30s for better compliance.

Specialized brags that the Tarmac SL9 S-Works is the fastest bike ever made, and it claims the S-Level is the second-fastest bike ever made. I can’t speak to that claim, but the SL9 S-Level checks all of the boxes for an aerodynamic road bike. It’s lightweight, it’s fast, it’s responsive, and it’s great on rollers and punchy climbs. And it’s nice to get a SRAM Red groupset without springing for the absolute top-of-the-line model. $10,500 is a hefty sum for a road bike, but I’d be a lot more willing to forego the weight savings from the lighter components (frame, wheels, seatpost, bottom bracket, and bearings) of the S-Works—and spend $3,500 less. The real selling point, though, is how often it made me want to go harder just for the fun of it. That little imp has a point: Zone 2 can wait until tomorrow.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations 2026: How the 32 franchises stack up

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For the first time, hockey team valuations have crossed the $4 billion threshold, with CNBC's NHL valuations led by the Toronto Maple Leafs, at $5.4 billion, the New York Rangers, at $4.7 billion, and the Montreal Canadiens, at $4.1 billion. The average NHL team is now worth $2.71 billion, 23% more than a year ago.

Don't be fooled by the lone NHL control sale to close over the past 12 months: the $1.8 billion sale of the Pittsburgh Penguins to the Hoffmann family. Although the transaction was approved by the league in June, the price was negotiated about 18 months ago.

Look instead at the latest minority stake transactions. In March the NHL approved the sale of a 12.5% stake in the Carolina Hurricanes by Tom Dundon in a transaction that valued the team at $2.66 billion. Scott Malkin and other shareholders are close to selling a 15% stake in the New York Islanders at a $3 billion valuation. Sports bankers tell CNBC these deals are representative of what control stakes in the two teams would sell for given the hot market for sports teams.

"Like the other major sports leagues there is a strong demand to own hockey teams because their media content values are high and there is a scarcity of teams," said Sal Galatioto, whose firm, Galatioto Sports Partners, advises clients on both the buy and sell sides of sports team transactions. "Capitalism and technology are creating more wealth and the NHL, like the NFL, NBA and MLB, is an industry with a very long product life cycle, a hedge against risk in AI."

The NHL's current national media rights deals with Walt Disney and Warner Bros. Discovery are worth a combined annual average of close to $650 million through the 2027-28 season, but given the league's strong television ratings last season, a big increase could be in the offing.

"The 2025-26 NHL regular season averaged 546,000 viewers across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports, up 25% from last year and the highest average in more than a decade," Sports Media Watch wrote. "The 25% increase is on the high end of a general rise in sports viewership that includes gains of 35% for the NBA, 19% for men's college basketball and 10% for the NFL."

Sports Media Watch also wrote that the 2026 NHL Stanley Cup Playoffs averaged 1.8 million viewers in the U.S. across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports. That's up 68% from 2025, and it's the highest average on record for the playoffs on American television, going back to 1994, it said.

The NHL is pondering adding teams in cities such as Houston and Atlanta, and another factor fueling NHL valuations is the belief that the expansion fees paid by the new teams would surpass $2 billion. The Seattle Kraken, the NHL's most recent expansion team, paid a $650 million fee to join the league for the 2021-22 season.

According to CNBC's figures, revenue for the league's 32 teams averaged $257 million for the 2025-26 season, 6% more than the previous year, while EBITDA — earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization — averaged $48 million per team, a decline of 11% due to arena expenses. The league's profitability should rebound as money spent on hospitality, concessions and merchandise stores boosts revenue.

In April 2025 the NHL and Rogers Communications agreed to a 12-year national Canadian media rights deal worth $7.79 billion, based on exchange rates as of Nov. 24, that will begin with the 2026-27 season. That's more than twice the amount the league is getting from its existing 12-year deal with Rogers. This will help push up the league's hockey-related revenue — which includes media rights, sponsorships, premium seating, tickets, concessions and parking — to around $7.25 billion for the 2026-27 season, 7% more than last season.

CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations are current enterprise values — equity plus net debt — calculated using revenue multiples, and include the economics of the team's arena, including non-NHL revenue that accrues to the team's owner. The valuations are meant to represent what a control stake valuation would go for in an arm's-length transaction and do not include the value of the arena or the real estate it sits on.

Revenue figures are net of revenue sharing and the 35% of home playoff non-premium gate revenue that goes toward the league's revenue-sharing pool. Revenue multiples are based on historical transaction prices, as well as what investors, league executives and bankers say about the current market.

CNBC values exclude other businesses related to the teams with separate financial statements, such as the Edmonton Oilers' mixed-use real estate project. For teams that sell their own merchandise, such as the Oilers, CNBC deducts the cost of goods sold from merchandise sales. Likewise, CNBC excludes the market value of affiliates/equity method investments to ensure consistent enterprise value-to-revenue and enterprise value-to-EBITDA multiples. Debt figures are the latest available and include both team debt and stadium debt.

CNBC incorporates the value of a pending new arena in our valuations if construction has begun and financing has been secured. For example, the $2.25 billion valuation for the Calgary Flames incorporates their new arena, which is scheduled to open in time for the 2027-28 season. Likewise, CNBC valuations anticipate a substantial increase in the NHL's next U.S. national broadcasting deal, which would begin with the 2028-29 season.

Revenue and EBITDA figures are for the 2025-26 season and net of revenue sharing and player escrow. CNBC presents revenue and operating income figures on a cash basis, rather than an accrual basis. CNBC figures are different from the league's official hockey-related revenue because we include non-NHL arena revenue that goes to the teams. When the owner of the arena also owns both the NHL team and NBA team that play in the same building — as is the case for several teams, including Toronto's Maple Leafs and Raptors, who are both owned by Rogers Communications and play in Scotiabank Arena — we divide revenue from non-sports events held at the arena evenly between the two teams.

All valuation figures are in U.S. dollars. The exchange rate for revenue and EBITDA is based on a one-year average, from June 2025 to June 2026, of CA$1 = US$0.72, and the exchange rate for team values and debt, as of Sept. 21, is CA$1 = US$0.71.

Sources for CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations include team owners, investors and executives, as well as sports bankers and league consultants; public documents such as stadium lease agreements, stadium authority budget audits and credit rating reports; and sponsorship and broadcasting industry executives. Certain figures were provided by league executives who spoke to CNBC on the condition of anonymity because agreements are private and they do not have permission to speak about them publicly. Figures that could not be confirmed with sources are CNBC estimates.

— CNBC's Jungwoo Ethan Kim contributed to this report.

Europe Haggles Over Who Will Represent It in Talks With Russia

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

After coveting a seat in the Ukraine war discussions, Europe is poised to get one. Deciding who should speak for the continent is its own negotiation.

Reporting from Dublin, where European leaders are meeting, and from an airfield near the city where Americans and Ukrainians debriefed on Thursday night.

European leaders have waited on the margins of negotiations to end Russia’s war in Ukraine for months, eyeing a seat at the table. Now they finally expect to get one, and they don’t know who should sit in it.

“If Europe could identify one person to speak on their behalf, that would be a bigger achievement than getting the Russians to include them in the negotiation,” Jared Kushner, President Trump’s son-in-law and a U.S. envoy, said on Thursday.

Mr. Kushner’s dig reflected a harsh — and, for Europe, potentially embarrassing — reality that internal divisions could cost European officials time and influence. Mr. Kushner and his fellow U.S. negotiator, Steve Witkoff, pushed this week to add Europe to the talks after Russia insisted during discussions in the United Arab Emirates that postwar security in Europe be a topic of negotiation.

Russian negotiators made it clear during the talks with Ukraine and the United States that as a condition of ending the war, they want Europe to change how it protects itself. Russia has a record of pushing to pare back NATO operations near its border.

European officials have rejected those suggestions, insisting that the continent, not the Kremlin, decides its security.

Humanoid Robots Are Coming to Factories. Who Is Responsible When They Fail?

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A robot may successfully complete a performance test and yet become a safety hazard if it faces an unexpected obstacle, a change in how it behaves due to a software update, or a real worker. The question is not whether humanoid robots can perform a task anymore, but how they can do it. Whether they can do it safely or not—and who is responsible if something goes wrong.

Agility Robotics and FORT Robotics announced a partnership in October to enhance the safety architecture of Agility’s Digit 5 humanoid robot. They have planned their approach by incorporating onboard safety systems and external interfaces, communications, and manual override. The development marks a significant shift: Robots have to be safe in human workplaces, and that means more than just the robot itself.

However, technology is not enough to answer the question of accountability. When a robot makes a mistake based on a faulty component misidentified by an AI system, the manufacturer, the software developer, the system integrator and the employer who is using the system could be held liable.

Traditional industrial robots are typically found in enclosed work cells, where they are isolated from workers by barriers and safety devices. Humanoid robots pose another challenge: they are supposed to navigate through environments constructed for humans, perform different tasks, and adapt to different environments.

That flexibility creates new risks. If a false detection occurs, it may lead to an unnecessary movement. A software update could alter a robot’s behavior. Changes in the factory layout may create hazards that were not present in previous tests.

Dana R. Wolfe, Vice President and Head of Industry, GFT Americas, told Unite AI to prevent unsafe interventions, multiple layers of protection are necessary, such as deterministic software guardrails, factory-specific confidence thresholds, sensor fusion and staged validation prior to deployment.

There is no way to completely avoid false-positive results, Wolfe said. Rather, manufacturers need to make sure that mistakes are not translated into unsafe physical movements.

Confidence thresholds and human authority should be part of the system’s safety architecture, as should be hardware interlocks, said Wolfe.

Current industrial robotics standards offer a valuable foundation, but are not necessarily the solution to all the problems presented by mobile humanoids.

The International Organization for Standardization (ISO) has published safety requirements for industrial robots, and ISO 10218-2 covers the integration of robots into complete systems.

These standards, however, do not address all applications or hazards of mobile robots working with humans.

Independent testing, scenario-based validation and phased workplace pilots are particularly important as industry practices evolve, said Wolfe.

Safety evaluations must also be repeated if changes in the AI model, software, movement restrictions, tasks or factory environment may render previous safety evaluations invalid.

After a major change, a robot that was safe in one set of conditions may not be safe in the new set of conditions.

It is impractical to monitor every decision an AI system makes, but when people are not involved in critical decisions, another challenge arises: accountability becomes more difficult to prove.

Adrian Figueroa Ramos, co-founder and CEO of Flexio and Routina, said that the focus of oversight should be on decisions that have the most uncertainty or risk.

“In today’s AI world, the volume of outputs is more than a human can process. Reviewing everything makes the human the bottleneck, but human judgment is still necessary for high-risk or low-confidence decisions,” he told Unite AI.

This is particularly true for physical AI. Human oversight should include named individuals with the power to override a system and ensure that decisions are documented and checked and not just click an approval button.

The accountability should be shared based on the control of each party over the system.

For instance, manufacturers are responsible for creating and documenting safety measures. Changes that may impact perception or movement should be tested by software developers.

The whole system needs to be validated in the intended environment by the integrator. Employers are responsible to train workers, ensure safeguards are in place and procedures are established for stopping unsafe operations.

Cybersecurity also matters. Operational technology can be vulnerable to sensor, software and external system issues connected to robots. A security failure that impacts physical behavior is a workplace safety problem, as well as an IT problem.

Humanoid robots are expected to become more flexible and productive in factories in the future. However, adoption will not be based solely on performance measures. There is a need for clear responsibility, independent validation and safeguards that are sustained throughout the working life of a robot.

The question is, can a robot make mistakes? It is, if the individuals who are using it have created a system that will hold them accountable for those errors.

Adlin Pertishya is a technology and business reporter who has worked for Finance Outlook India, Silicon India, StartupBeat and others.

Why Agentic AI Projects Get Canceled, and What an Eight-Week Pilot Gets Right

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A customer asks for a refund, and the agent finds the order in the CRM, checks the returns policy and drafts a reply in under a minute. Then it stalls, because the refund approval sits in a finance system it was never connected to and the customer’s address doesn’t match between two databases. Gaps of this kind rarely surface in a demo, yet they decide how much an agent costs to run and whether anyone can trust it, which puts the bigger risk in agentic AI inside the business the agent is dropped into rather than inside the model.

Gartner expects more than 40% of agentic AI projects to be canceled by the end of 2027, pointing to rising costs, unclear business value and weak risk controls. Read closely, that forecast is about how these projects get approved on the strength of a demo, with little view of what the work costs today or who owns the outcome.

The question I’d put to any leadership team is whether the business can support this agent doing the work every day, at a cost and level of risk it can live with. Answering it means looking hard at what I call the work after automation, meaning everything people still have to do once the agent has finished its part, and an eight-week pilot is a practical way to measure it before committing to a full rollout.

An agent rarely works on its own, since even a simple-looking task touches customer records, a couple of business applications and usually an approval step, and every connection limits what the agent can see and do. Where records are inconsistent or nobody is quite sure who signs off, the agent inherits the confusion along with the task.

Routine cases are seldom the problem; exceptions are, because that’s where operating costs pile up, and they’re precisely the part a demo is built to avoid. Deloitte’s 2026 research found that only 5% of organizations rate their business processes as highly prepared for AI agents, a number more telling than any model benchmark because it suggests the AI is ready well before most processes are.

Timing how fast an agent produces an output is the easiest way to overstate its value, since the figure that matters is how much effort the finished task takes. Say a request takes an employee 20 minutes to process, and the agent gets the first pass down to two, which looks like an 18-minute saving right up until someone spends 15 minutes checking the result and fixing errors. Three minutes saved is a thin return once the cost of running the agent goes on the other side of the ledger.

McKinsey’s analysis of a banking customer-service agent shows the same pattern at scale: tokens made up 20–25% of variable operating costs and human oversight 70–75%, so most of the money sat outside the model. Without a baseline in place before anyone writes code, a pilot can show plenty of technical progress and still leave the investment case untested. At a minimum, the business owner needs a clear answer on each of these points:

Adoption usually gets filed under change management, although in my experience it’s settled much earlier, by where the agent sits in the workflow. A global professional services company had built agents for support and compliance work, and its support agent could pull information from several internal systems, but people had to open a separate application to use it and mostly didn’t bother. Once the same workflow was moved into the existing support platform, it appeared inside the ticket associates were already working on, and a compliance screening agent went the same way, built into Salesforce so that it ran whenever a case was created.

Nothing about the agents themselves changed; their position in the workflow did. Associates already had a reason to open a ticket or create a case, so meeting them at that moment asked nothing extra of them. Placement is therefore an architecture decision as much as an adoption one, because the agent needs context where the work happens, and its output has to feed the next step without someone copying it across by hand.

An agent that can only read information carries one kind of risk, and one that can change records or email customers carries another entirely, so the controls have to follow what it’s actually allowed to do. Excessive permissions and leaking sensitive data are the familiar risks, but prompt injection deserves the closest attention: instructions hidden in a document or an email that the agent picks up while doing a perfectly legitimate job.

Gartner’s May 2026 guidance on agent governance breaks autonomy into four levels, which gives a practical way to decide how tight the controls need to be:

At every level, an agent needs its own identity, permissions limited to its task and a record of each action it takes. Organizations also need rules for how data is handled and retained, clarity on what reaches outside model providers, and someone with the authority to pause the agent and pull its access the moment something looks wrong.

Each of these controls carries a running cost, since wider permissions, extra monitoring and frequent approvals all add up over time. The right level of autonomy is therefore an economic decision as well as a security one, and it sits wherever the agent delivers a worthwhile outcome at a risk the business can accept.

Plenty of teams start with the technology and go looking for a problem when the better starting point is a process whose cost or delays are already obvious to the people doing the work.

A global expert-network company we worked with fit that description: its team was matching survey requests to experts across three systems that didn’t talk to each other, checking profiles, past participation and compliance by hand for every match. We built an agent that combined semantic search with that participation history and the compliance rules, and in production mismatch tickets dropped 80%, mapping accuracy rose 40% and the team got back 22% of its bandwidth.

The project worked because the problem was specific, with scattered information, manual stitching and match quality that could be measured before and after. Specificity also helps answer whether a process needs an agent at all. Work governed by fixed rules is usually cheaper to handle with conventional automation, and work that mostly involves finding and summarizing information is often well served by an AI assistant, while agents earn their extra cost and risk when the job means reading context, choosing between permitted actions and chaining several steps toward an outcome.

The whole pilot hangs on one commercial question: can this agent improve the process enough to justify running it at scale? Eight weeks gives enough room to test the business case, the technology and the organization’s ability to manage what it builds.

Start by documenting the process as it runs today, from volume and completion time to error rates, rework and cost. Note which systems are involved, who owns each stage and which exceptions need a human, then get the business owner and the technical team to agree on the one outcome the agent is supposed to improve. Decision point: keep going once the problem, the baseline, the owner and the success measures are clear.

This is where the agent’s limits get set, covering its identity, permissions, data access, approved tools and degree of autonomy. Test it against prompt injection, unauthorized access, sensitive-data exposure and tool misuse, and put logging and incident response in place along with a way to suspend the agent or revoke its access. Decision point: keep going once the controls and intervention steps have actually been tested and everyone knows what the agent is allowed to do.

Now give it realistic work, including incomplete records, conflicting information, odd requests and the occasional system failure. Track how many tasks it completes, how accurate it is and how much correcting people have to do, then work out what a successfully completed task really costs once model usage, integrations, monitoring and review are all counted. Decision point: move to live testing when the agent hits the agreed thresholds and the team understands both its running cost and the ways it fails.

Give it to a small group of employees and a controlled slice of real work, with the old process still there for anything the agent can’t handle. Watch whether people actually use it, whether it saves them effort and whether they trust what it produces.

At the end, put the results next to the baseline from week one and look at productivity, accuracy, adoption, how often people had to step in, total operating cost and any security risks still open. The call is then to scale, fix specific gaps and retest, or stop, and a pilot that shows the agent is too expensive or too unreliable for the job has still done its work, because it has prevented a much bigger investment built on guesswork.

Counting how many tasks an agent processes says very little, since a system can churn through thousands of requests and still leave employees verifying, correcting or finishing most of them. Cost per successfully completed task, read alongside accuracy and exception rates, gives a far better picture of whether the process has improved end to end.

Those same measures become the case for giving an agent more room. One that reliably handles routine requests inside agreed limits can be trusted to act on them alone, while one that keeps meeting ambiguous cases keeps a human approving those decisions, with permissions reviewed as the evidence builds.

An agent that works well for one team will sooner or later have to deal with other departments, applications and approval chains. Scaling it takes clear ownership, consistent access controls and a way of handling exceptions, along with a budget for keeping the agent current as the processes around it change.

The decision underneath all of this is how much responsibility the organization is ready to hand over, and it has to rest on evidence from real work rather than a good demo. Leadership should come out of the eight weeks knowing what the agent can handle, where people still need to step in and what it will cost to run. The enterprises that pull ahead with agentic AI will be the ones that design for the work after automation from the first week and fund agents on the tasks they actually complete.

Bhupendra Chopra is the Chief Revenue Officer and Co-Founder of Kanerika, a data and AI solutions company. He works with enterprises on data platform modernization, AI adoption, and moving AI projects from pilot to production. His experience covers Microsoft Fabric, Databricks, Power BI migrations, data governance, and agentic AI.

Before co-founding Kanerika, Bhupendra held roles at Informatica, Oracle, and TCS. He has completed the Stanford Leadership Learning Program and an executive program at IIM Indore. He is based in Austin, Texas.

Digital Credit: Comparing The Six Bitcoin Preferred Stocks

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A simple four-part investor framework for evaluating Digital Credit.

Digital credit is an emerging asset class that transforms corporate Bitcoin balance sheets into income producing securities. Strategy pioneered the market with five perpetual preferred equity instruments: STRK, STRF, STRD, STRC, and STRE (the first four are listed in the U.S.). Strive subsequently introduced SATA and SATS, bringing the category to six U.S. instruments across two issuers.

These securities offer investors preferred equity (read: credit-like) exposure to companies whose balance sheets are dominated by Bitcoin. Their perpetual structure means investors principally rely on recurring dividends and the continued financial strength of the issuer. Unlike term debt, there is no maturation date at which the full principal is ever paid back.

Evaluating digital credit requires examining four characteristics: creditworthiness, investor protections, liquidity, and dividend profile.

This article explains all four dimensions and then offers a ranking for the six digital credit instruments along these lines. None of this should be taken as investment advice—it is only a systematic framework for analyzing digital credit.

Creditworthiness measures an issuer’s ability to support its financial obligations.

Traditional credit analysis focuses on earnings, free cash flow, and refinancing capacity. Companies with strong cash generation can service interest and repay maturing debt. Digital credit issuers today derive financial strength primarily from their Bitcoin reserves, placing greater emphasis on net asset coverage. In the future we could certainly see digital credit issuers that also have significant cash flows on top of Bitcoin holdings, which would add to creditworthiness.

The central question is how much Bitcoin supports each dollar of credit obligations. One useful measure is the BTC Rating, which compares the dollar value of Bitcoin reserves with the outstanding credit, accounting for the seniority of each credit instrument. Cash reserves can further strengthen creditworthiness by reducing net obligations.

For example, a 5x BTC Rating indicates five dollars of Bitcoin supporting every dollar of notional credit claims. Higher ratings provide greater protection against Bitcoin price declines.

Seniority matters because instruments from the same issuer can have different claims on the same asset base. A senior preferred instrument benefits from greater coverage than a junior preferred instrument: all senior claims are included in the BTC Rating calculation of a junior instrument.

Creditworthiness also depends on corporate governance and capital allocation. Management must preserve sufficient Bitcoin coverage while funding recurring dividends. Issuing additional credit increases dividend obligations, while assets accumulation (Bitcoin or cash) strengthen the asset base. Excessive leverage, poor treasury management, or sustained Bitcoin declines can weaken coverage over time.

Investors must therefore evaluate both their assumptions about Bitcoin’s long term performance and management’s ability to maintain a sustainable capital structure.

Investor protections determine what happens when an issuer fails to pay its promised dividends.

While creditworthiness concerns an issuer’s ability to pay, investor protections govern the consequences of missed payments, and more specifically the compensation investors may receive for delayed income.

The first distinction is cumulative versus noncumulative dividends. Cumulative dividends continue accumulating when payments are skipped, preserving the investor’s claim to unpaid distributions. Noncumulative dividends permit the issuer to permanently forgo a payment.

The second distinction is the treatment of unpaid dividends. Some instruments preserve unpaid amounts without additional compensation. Others compound arrears at the regular dividend rate. The strongest protections impose penalty rates, which cause unpaid dividends to compound at progressively higher rates as deferrals continue.

Penalty rates create financial incentives for management to maintain regular payments. They also compensate investors for the additional risk and delay associated with missed distributions.

Other investor protections may include restrictions on payments to junior shareholders, preferred shareholder voting rights or director elections, and dedicated dividend reserves.

These provisions establish the contractual guardrails surrounding dividend payments. Stronger protections increase the cost of deferral and improve the investor’s position to receive income.

Liquidity measures how easily investors can enter and exit positions without materially affecting market prices. This is especially important for digital credit, which does not mature and pay back principal on a fixed date. A liquid market is how an investor would “get his money back” if he wants to exit his position.

Digital credit has developed substantial secondary market liquidity, particularly in its leading instruments. The six U.S.-listed digital credit tickers are among the most actively traded preferred equity securities in the U.S. markets.

Two metrics help investors evaluate liquidity.

Absolute trading volume measures the total dollar value traded over a given period. Greater volume accommodates larger transactions and makes it easier for institutions to establish or liquidate positions.

Relative liquidity, or turnover, measures trading volume as a percentage of an instrument’s market capitalization. It captures how actively a security trades relative to its outstanding size.

For example, an instrument with $50 million in daily volume and a $1 billion market capitalization has 5% daily turnover. Another instrument trading $100 million daily with a $10 billion market capitalization has only 1% turnover. The second instrument offers greater absolute trading capacity, while the first experiences substantially greater trading activity relative to its size.

Absolute volume facilitates large transactions, while relative turnover reveals the level of trading activity relative to the capital outstanding.

Dividend profile describes the characteristics of the income an investor receives. Three main considerations are the dividend rate policy, payment frequency, and dividend yield.

The first distinction is between fixed and variable dividends. Fixed dividends establish a predetermined annual payment per share. Investors know the nominal income they are entitled to receive, allowing them to plan around a predictable cash flow stream. Variable dividends allow issuers to adjust the payout rate over time. These adjustments can respond to market interest rates, investor demand, or the trading price of the instrument. Fixed dividends prioritize income predictability for investors with reduced flexibility for issuers. Variable dividends provide flexibility for issuers but less predictability for investors.

The second consideration is payment frequency. Traditional preferred securities generally pay quarterly. Digital credit has introduced monthly, twice monthly, and daily dividend schedules. More frequent payments improve cash flow availability, accelerate reinvestment opportunities, and reduce the concentration of distributions around individual payment dates. After January 1 2027, all six instruments will be doing daily dividends.

The third consideration, and perhaps the most important out of anything mentioned in this article, is the raw dividend yield. This is the annualized income received divided by the trading price of the credit. A higher yield is generally preferred, but it also must be contextualized against risks and other considerations.

The following framework ranks the six instruments relative to one another. Rank 1 represents the strongest position and rank 6 the weakest, with ties where instruments share identical characteristics.

Creditworthiness and investor protections receive overall rankings. Liquidity is segmented into absolute trading volume and turnover. Dividend profile is segmented into rate certainty and yield. While each category’s segments can be evaluated together as a composite metric based on the investor’s own needs, I am choosing to segment them for more precision. I have also thrown out payment frequency since all six will be daily in less than two months.

For dividend rate certainty, fixed dividends rank ahead of variable dividends because their nominal payments are predetermined. This ranking specifically measures income predictability.

Equal signs indicate tied rankings. Liquidity rankings are based on average daily trading volumes from November 2–11, 2026, and turnover is calculated using the latest reported shares outstanding.

Creditworthiness: Strategy’s preferreds rank ahead of Strive’s because of their greater asset coverage. Within each issuer, seniority determines the ranking. STRF has the highest BTC Rating because it is Strategy’s most senior preferred, while SATS has the lowest coverage because it ranks below SATA in Strive’s capital structure.

Investor protections: SATA and STRF rank first and second because unpaid dividends compound at escalating penalty rates and holders can elect directors following prolonged dividend deferrals. SATA’s higher penalty rate ceiling and dividend reserve give it the edge. STRC and STRK both compound unpaid dividends at the regular dividend rate. I rank STRC ahead of STRK based on its higher current compounding rate, although STRK also gives holders the right to elect directors after prolonged dividend deferrals. SATS has cumulative dividends, but unpaid dividends do not compound. STRD ranks last because its dividends are noncumulative, meaning any skipped dividends are permanently forgone. Investors also have no right to bring on directors.

Of the six, STRC and SATA are variable rate instruments and the remaining four are fixed rate. This makes the dividend rate policy pretty straightforward.

The remaining columns—volume, turnover, and yield—are trackable numbers. For instance, here are turnover and volume numbers from the BFC Digital Credit Dashboard:

Each of the six digital credit instruments has a different set of tradeoffs. Investors who prioritize creditworthiness may prefer STRF, while those who prioritize dividend certainty and yield may prefer SATS.

The relative importance of each of these dimensions depends on the investor’s own needs. As digital credit continues to develop, investors can use this framework to evaluate new instruments and determine which ones are best suited to their objectives.

Baselane Review: How to Stop Landlord Burnout From Costing You $800,000+

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Rental property is how I broke free. The rent from my San Francisco properties was one of the foundations that let me leave banking in 2012 at age 34. Without it, I’d probably still be waking up at 5 am to get to the office by 5:45 am and leaving after 6 pm.

I’ve been a landlord since 2005. I’ve had good tenants, great tenants, and tenants who refused to take their trash with them during the final walkthrough.

I’m forever grateful for owning rental property. But the older I get, the less I enjoy being a landlord.

It’s not the math. It’s the tenant headaches, the turnover, and the maintenance. The 9 pm leaky faucet text. The final walkthrough that starts 30 minutes late. At 49 with two young kids, my time is worth more and my patience is worth less.

2025 tested me with three tenant turnovers. Each one meant a deposit to return, walls to paint, a vacancy to absorb, and a new tenant to find. Not long after, I sold one of my rentals. Earning 100% passive income feels better.

But selling also feels like giving up. Owning rental property is a war of attrition. The longer you hold, the more wealth you usually build. I had hoped to hold on until my kids were adults, and I didn’t.

Selling isn’t free either. Commissions, transfer taxes, capital gains, and depreciation recapture take a big bite. Then you lose the rent check and future appreciation on a property you’ll likely never buy back at the same price.

Semi-passive income you can live with beats optimized income you can’t wait to dump. You can’t automate away a difficult tenant or a burst pipe. But you can automate a lot of what surrounds them. That’s where Baselane comes in.

For 21 years, I’ve tracked my rental finances with a spreadsheet and emails. Old school. With three rentals left in San Francisco, it kind of works. I know my numbers, more or less.

But add another property, an LLC, a partner, or a short-term rental, and a DIY system starts to creak. Debits and credits slip through the cracks.

Four rentals is why I sold one last year. I couldn’t comfortably handle all the maintenance and management myself. And I didn’t want to pay a property manager 10% of rent or more to do a worse job than me.

Since Baselane is sponsoring this post, I opened a free account for one of my SF rentals to see how it compares. Here’s what stood out.

Baselane is a free business bank account, bookkeeping software, and rent collection app rolled into one login, built for landlords. Think business checking plus QuickBooks plus Venmo for rent, except it knows what a Schedule E is.

Banking and books live in the same system, so you never reconcile. Baselane is a fintech, not a bank. Deposits are held at Thread Bank, Member FDIC.

One of the first rules of landlording is to keep rental money separate from personal money. One of the second rules is that it gets messy fast once you own more than one property.

With Baselane, you can open unlimited checking and savings accounts under one login. One per property, one per LLC, one for reserves, however you actually run your portfolio. There are no monthly maintenance fees and no minimum balances, which matters once you're running a handful of accounts.

You can also issue physical and virtual debit cards with spending limits. If you pay contractors, cleaners, or a handyman, you can hand someone a card capped at, say, $1,500 for materials instead of fronting cash and chasing receipts.

The reserve accounts earn interest. Currently 2.88%. Letting a five-figure repair reserve sit in a checking account earning nothing is a donation to your bank.

If you try it, start with one property. Move its rent and its bills over, get comfortable, then decide about the rest.

Plenty of banks let you open separate accounts. What's different here is that every transaction gets tagged to a property and a Schedule E category as it happens.

Here's why that matters. Before my old Marina tenants moved in, I spent about $2,500 and many hours fixing up the yard. They agreed to hire a gardener twice a month. They never did. Before they moved out, I paid my landscaper $1,400 including materials to restore it, plus two days of my own labor.

Those are deductible repair and maintenance expenses. But only if you record them against the right property and can find the receipts a year later. With one property, I can do that myself. With five, it becomes a part-time job.

On the Smart plan, you set rules once, like “payments to my landscaper go to the SF rental under Repairs,” and an AI assistant handles the rest. You can snap a photo of a receipt and it matches it to the transaction.

This is the part that speaks to my real pain point: tenant headaches. It is also my favorite reason for using Baselane.

I rented my Marina house to five tenants from 2015 to 2017 for about $8,200 a month. The lease charged a $250 late fee after the 4th. Rent was late eight times in 24 months. I waived the fee every single time.

That’s $2,000 I left on the table because I’m a softie who dislikes confrontation. Their method of paying was walking into my bank branch and depositing directly into my account. One July 4th weekend, rent showed up on the 10th. That’s difficult to deal with when my mortgage is due by the 5th of each month, resulting in cash flow issues.

That awkwardness wore me down. In 2017, I sold the Marina house for about $2.75 million to simplify my life.

Thanks to the AI boom pushing San Francisco prices higher, that house is likely worth $800,000 to $1 million more today. Sure, I reinvested the proceeds, so the true opportunity cost is lower. But it still stings.

If I’d had a system to collect rent, send reminders, and enforce late fees without me playing bad cop, I would have held on. Selling out of fatigue can be one of the most expensive decisions a landlord makes.

That’s what Baselane does. Invoices, reminders, and late fees go out on schedule. Tenants can pay by ACH or card and set up autopay. Software doesn’t feel guilty enforcing a lease you both signed. Ultimately, Baselane removes friction which increases a rental property owner’s ability to hold on and build more wealth.

After 21 years, I've also learned that 99% of tenants want as little contact with their landlord as possible. The feeling is mutual. A system that runs rent in the background means fewer awkward conversations, and fewer awkward conversations means a happier landlord who's less tempted to sell.

Two catches. Tenants who pay by card pay a 3.49% fee, so steer them to ACH. And if your current rent collection works fine, then great. Baselane's value is rent, banking, and books living in one place.

If you run short-term rentals, payouts from Airbnb, Vrbo, or Booking.com can take several business days to clear at a normal bank. Across multiple listings, that's a constant gap between money earned and money available.

Baselane offers faster payouts when you use it as your deposit account on booking platforms, with each listing feeding its own account. Next-day payouts are available for free users.

A healthy bank balance doesn't mean a property is performing. You might have a big repair coming, insurance up 20%, or expenses sitting in the wrong account.

I recently ran the numbers on my San Francisco rental for a post on whether to raise the rent when tenants strike it rich. Rent is $10,250 a month, roughly $1,250 below market, because I honor a three-year rent schedule capped at about 3% a year. One vacant month wipes out about eight months of that discount. And a 2.5% annual increase only covers about 80% of my rising insurance and basic maintenance costs.

I know those numbers because I track them myself. Most landlords never sit down and run them.

Baselane builds income, expense, cash flow, and net operating income reports for each property or your whole portfolio, because every transaction is already tagged. No building spreadsheets from scratch.

With one rental and a spreadsheet you trust, you may not need this. With several properties or LLCs, it's the difference between knowing and guessing whether a property is worth keeping.

Tax season is where sloppy books come back to bite. You end up gathering records, digging up receipts, and trying to remember what a charge from eight months ago was for.

Because Baselane categorizes everything during the year, you can download a tax package with Schedule E reports, income statements, and transaction ledgers with receipts for each property and entity. Then you hand it to your CPA or use it to file yourself.

If you have one rental and a DIY system that works, you don't need it. But the Core plan is free, so there's little downside to trying it.

Baselane makes the most sense if you own several rentals, hold properties in LLCs, or self-manage. Property managers have quoted me fees ranging from 10% to 20% of rent. On a $10,250 rental, a 10% fee is $1,250 a month. Baselane Smart, its premium version is just $20.

That's not apples to apples. Baselane won't fix your garbage disposal or talk to your tenants. But if you self-manage, the administrative half of the job gets a lot lighter.

It's also worth a look if you're on the fence about selling a rental because you're worn out. Lighten the load before you list it and pay all those exorbitant fees. And if you're buying your next property, set up clean systems first. They're easier to build with one property than to retrofit with five.

Core is free and includes unlimited accounts, debit cards with spend controls, rent collection with automated reminders and late fees, property-level tracking, and the accountant tax package.

Smart is $20 a month with a 30-day free trial and adds AI auto-tagging, receipt matching, auto-transfers to maintain target balances, faster rent deposits, and balance sheet reports.

Compare that to what you may already pay. Some business bank accounts charge $5 to $25 a month in maintenance fees. At $15 a month across five accounts, that's $900 a year just to keep them open. Baselane charges no maintenance fees, no minimums, and ACH transfers and incoming wires are free.

No product is perfect, and pretending otherwise insults your intelligence.

Rental property helped me break free due to the semi-passive income it generates. But a rental only builds wealth if you can stand to keep owning it.

The headaches that make me want to sell are tenants, turnover, and maintenance. Baselane doesn't make those entirely disappear. What it does is take the money side of landlording, rent collection, late fees, accounts, and records, and put it on autopilot. Less friction overall means more energy for the parts that actually need a human.

My plan going forward is simple. Own fewer, better rentals and make each one as close to passive as possible. Let the private real estate side of my portfolio be 100% passive. And spend the time I save being a full-time dad.

If I’d had automated rent and late fees with my Marina tenants, I’d have $2,000 more and far fewer awkward conversations. I also might still own the house today. That’s at least an $800,000 lesson in letting small annoyances drive big decisions.

Selling a rental is permanent. Making one easier to own isn't.

If you own rental property and want it to feel a little more passive, check out Baselane here. The Core plan is free, so the only cost of trying it is an afternoon.

Have you ever sold a rental property because you were tired of managing it, not because the numbers stopped working? Do you regret it? What's the one landlord headache that would push you to sell: tenants, turnover, maintenance, or the paperwork? Do you track your rental finances yourself or hand them off? And have you ever waived a late fee you were fully entitled to collect?

What should real estate investors look for when choosing a bank? Look for property-specific accounts, integrated rent collection, automated bookkeeping, and tax prep support. Multi-property investors benefit most from platforms that organize transactions by property and entity automatically. Security deposit tracking also helps keep tenant funds clearly separated.

Can real estate investors open a business bank account online? Yes. Digital platforms like Baselane let you open accounts entirely online with no branch visit. Traditional banks may require in-person verification depending on their policies and your business structure.

Do any banking platforms for real estate investors offer high-yield savings? Digital-first platforms generally pay more on savings than traditional banks. Baselane pays a tiered APY on savings balances that exceeds the national average for business accounts. Traditional banks typically pay far less on business savings.

How do integrated banking and bookkeeping platforms help with taxes? Platforms like Baselane categorize transactions by property and IRS Schedule E category throughout the year. That cuts manual work at tax time and produces Schedule E reports and transaction ledgers you can hand to your accountant or use to file yourself.

Does Baselane support multiple properties? Yes. Baselane supports bookkeeping across multiple rental properties for U.S.-based entities, including single-member and multi-member LLCs, corporations, partnerships, and sole proprietorships. Banking and rent collection sync directly with bookkeeping, and you can connect external bank accounts and import historical transactions. See the pricing page for the full feature list.

What reports can real estate investors generate? Portfolio-level and property-level reports including net operating income, net cash flow, rent roll, tenant ledger, total inflows and outflows, transaction ledger with receipts, balance sheet, Schedule E, and income statement. Reports export for your accountant, lender, or tax filing.

Protein Startups Are Selling Lots Of Snacks And Drinks. Our Sample Tasting Found Most Neither Great Nor Horrible

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In a perfect world, we would all eat meals and snacks that are convenient, taste great and provide optimal nutrition.

In reality, however, there are tradeoffs. We crave ice cream, chips and candy bars, which make carbs and fat easy to over-consume. Meeting protein goals, meanwhile, can require conscientious effort, particularly for those, like your author, who aren’t keen on eating lots of meat.

Fortunately, as we chronicled earlier this week, both startups and established food brands are making it simpler than ever to up protein intake. From sodas to snack bars to ice cream, it’s now possible to get a fix of this coveted macronutrient with foods and drinks more commonly associated with sugar, starch and fat.

The looming question is: Do they taste good? To find out, we hit up a local Target for a selection of protein foods sold by venture-backed startups. I and a 10-year-old then subjected them to a taste test.

The verdict? Most of this stuff is indeed edible and drinkable, albeit almost never nearly as tasty as the more protein-deficient alternatives, with just one exception.

No. 1: David Vanilla Bean Frozen Dessert: Anyone who buys or consumes this particular ice cream alternative will be doing so for the nutritional content, not the taste. A single pint packs in a whopping 30 grams of protein and has just 210 calories. The texture is also on point, creamy and easy to spoon, with a distinct vanilla flavor.

That said, you can’t hide the fact that this is not a product made with your typical ice cream ingredients. Those who don’t like artificial sweeteners or the flavor and texture of whey protein will want to pass. For myself and my co-taster, however, it was a reasonably appealing eating experience. Not as good as traditional ice cream, but not bad for a high-protein food.

Made by: Medici Brands (David parent company).

No. 2 SkyPop Protein Soda: The nutritional stats for SkyPop Protein Soda are also remarkable. A 12-ounce can has just 45 calories, with 10 grams of protein and a single gram of carbohydrates. It’s also properly fizzy, and makes a satisfying pop when you open the can.

But what about flavor? Our local store only had the Ice Pop flavor in stock and it was, in both tasters’ view, not horrible but not something we’d plan to buy regularly. I could see consuming it when on a clear fluids diet, as is the case before some medical procedures, as it’d be one of the few available sources of protein.

No. 3: Khloud Protein Popcorn: Unlike many of our other featured foods, Khloud Protein Popcorn isn’t a super protein-rich offering. A serving contains 7 grams of protein and 160 calories, most of which come from carbs and fat. Still, its protein count is higher than other packaged popcorn, which typically has maybe 2 protein grams in a comparable serving.

Overall, the tasters’ conclusion, from our trial of White Cheddar Khloud, was that this is a decent snack food. The flavor of milk protein and whey protein concentrate was palpable, but not overwhelming. It’s not exactly a snack food to savor, but for something to casually munch at one’s desk or while watching a show, it served its purpose.

No. 4 Mosh: In the third season of “The White Lotus”, Mosh co-founder Patrick Schwarzenegger plays a fitness and finance bro who makes a daily high-protein shake that other family members find revolting. Perhaps this was inspired by Mosh, the brain-supporting protein bar startup he launched with his mother, Maria Shriver.

The Mosh bar, which we purchased in the peanut butter cup flavor, crams 20 grams of protein, 6 grams of fiber, plus brain-supporting ingredients like creatine and lion’s mane, into a compact, shelf-stable bar. It’s an impressive feat. Yet while our brains’ may have appreciated the nutrition and supplement boost the Mosh bar provided, our taste buds decidedly did not.

I’d put Mosh in the functional food category. It’s something that you might eat if you wanted that particular nutrient mix. But it’s not something to consume for pleasure.

No. 5 Cadootz Crackers: We tried two varieties of Cadootz Crackers — cheddar and sea salt — and walked away neither enthused nor repulsed.

For people interested in a gluten- and seed oil-free small snack cracker with about 5 grams of protein per serving, Cadootz rises to the occasion. It’s got a nice crunch and decent flavor.

As someone who’s not particularly seeking to avoid gluten and seed oils or to get a modest protein bump from my crackers, I’ll probably pass on follow-on purchases. But for its target audience, Cadootz should serve well.

No. 6 Frozen One: For those who thought the David frozen dessert was a marvel of protein engineering, the folks at Frozen One would like a word. Their protein ice cream has a whopping 40 grams of protein in each pint.

Yes, but how does it taste? Not that bad, according to our two-person tasting team. We tried both the chocolate and apple pie varieties, preferring the former. Like the David offering, Frozen One tastes like a product made with milk protein concentrate and sugar substitutes. You won’t mistake it for Ben and Jerry’s. But if the goal is to consume a steak-like quantity of protein in the form factor of a pint of ice cream, it does its job.

No. 7 Brami Protein Pasta: We saved the best for last. That, unexpectedly, was Brami Protein Pasta, a noodle made from semolina and lupini beans that boasts about 70% more protein than standard pasta and more than double the fiber.

With about 12 grams of protein for a 200 calorie serving, Brami isn’t a dense protein source. But, with the right topping, it’s enough to transform a typically starchy noodle meal into something reasonably protein rich.

Most notably, however, Brami tastes quite good, with no discernable weird aftertaste or texture issues. I made both standard and Brami noodles to taste the difference. For seconds, I went with the Brami.

Outlook outage continues as Microsoft flags 'encouraging recovery progress'

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Microsoft suffered a widespread outage on Thursday and Friday, causing disruption to millions of its customers as engineers scrambled to fix one of its biggest-ever server issues.

Connectivity issues began shortly before 1 p.m. ET on Thursday and continued into Friday, with Outlook and Teams, along with multiple Microsoft 365 services such as Sharepoint and Copilot, affected. Numerous external services that connect with Microsoft products such as Outlook Calendar were also hit.

In an update shortly after 7 a.m. ET, Microsoft continued to warn that users may be unable to connect to multiple Microsoft 365 products, including Outlook on either web or desktop and many services that interact with email.

However, it said it was making "encouraging recovery progress in Europe," with service availability and email delivery improving for some users. It said service is being "gradually restored" in the Asia Pacific and that the situation in North America was "improving." Intermittent issues are expected as recovery efforts continue.

Email and related tools were unavailable for Microsoft users on Thursday afternoon and evening, throughout the Asian business day on Friday and across the European and early U.S. morning.

Engineering teams have been taking affected systems offline for fixes and bringing them back online in stages. They are prioritizing efforts to preserve emails delayed during the recovery process and reduce email queues and backlogs, extending the timeout window, whereby delayed emails continue to attempt delivery, to 48 hours to help protect messages.

Microsoft says it has not detected any malicious activity causing the outage, which it initially blamed on a traffic surge in part of its infrastructure and issues with a component responsible for processing server health.

The company said in its initial updates on Thursday that the connectivity problems related to infrastructure "elevated load across multiple regions," caused by a "number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable; straining the remaining infrastructure." Some servers were failing automated health checks, it said.

Microsoft shares were around 2% lower in premarket trade.

Governments and businesses in Asia-Pacific and Europe said they were monitoring the situation, which was causing widespread disruption.

The U.K.'s National Health Service said the outage was affecting some of its services but that patients should attend appointments as normal. Calls "may be subject to long waits" as a result of disruption to the organization, a hospital group serving South West London and the English county of Surrey said on X.

Earnings for Swiss luxury goods giant Richemont were slightly delayed on Friday due to the outage. Electric truck maker Workhorse Group, which was expected to report third-quarter results on Nov. 12, rescheduled the release to Nov. 16, citing business disruptions caused by the global Microsoft outage.

Air India said it had "temporarily rerouted" emails so it could continue to receive customer messages, adding that its flight operations were not affected.

Australian Home Affairs and Cyber Security Minister Tony Burke said in a press conference that Microsoft "believe they are dealing in some ways similar to what we saw with respect to CrowdStrike where there is an update that has gone awry," Reuters reported.

A fault with an update issued by cybersecurity company CrowdStrike caused one of the world's biggest IT system outages in 2024, hitting airlines, banks and more,

Microsoft reported in 2025 that Copilot, its collection of AI assistants and tools, had more than 100 million monthly active users.

Amazon Web Services and Google users also reported outages on the site on Thursday.

Amazon wrote on X that services were "operating normally" and an "unrelated disruption elsewhere on the internet is prompting some inaccurate news reports." The company referred users to its health dashboard for "accurate data."

At 4 p.m. ET Thursday, Microsoft said it had "validated an alternative service health check method" that brought some affected servers back online.

"We're now applying this change more broadly," Microsoft wrote. "We're also preparing automated recovery systems to continually detect and restart unresponsive servers, to maintain service stability while this change progresses."
